`````
# Changes by Grok Bot

Active branch: `grokbot/blog-on-latest`. Static blog overlay for the cPanel site, generated by `tools/blog` (see `tools/blog/README.md`). Nothing here is deployed automatically.

## 20 Nov 2026: batch 31 (patellofemoral pain post, DEKA sources)

- New post (data-only, `"format": "evidence"`): **Patellofemoral (kneecap) pain: the evidence**
  (`/blog/patellofemoral-pain-and-exercise`) in en, ar (RTL), hi, es, zh, ja, gu, with hreflang (7 + x-default), cards in all 7 blog
  indexes, 7 sitemap URLs, and a link in the fat-loss page's "Read next". Data: `tools/blog/data/posts/patellofemoral-pain-and-exercise.json`,
  top of `order.json`. See also: leg strength and knee health; strength training for runners. Topic: item 2 of the topic queue
  (item 1, low back pain with NICE NG59, is already covered by `best-core-exercises-for-lower-back-health`).
- Sources (9), each opened and checked for this post: Willy 2019 JOSPT clinical practice guideline (summary of recommendations, prevalence
  section, grade definitions; read from the full-text PDF because jospt.org blocks automated reads), Collins 2018 consensus (PubMed abstract),
  van der Heijden 2015 Cochrane review (PubMed abstract), Lack 2015 (abstract), Smith 2018 (abstract), Neal 2019 (abstract), Lankhorst 2016
  (abstract), NHS knee pain page (read 20 Nov 2026; last reviewed 21 December 2023), Riebe 2015 ACSM screening (abstract).
  Considered and not used: Crossley 2016 Manchester consensus part 1 (PubMed record has no abstract; the guideline's definition is used instead).
- Content: who was studied, what was compared, effect sizes and the guideline's own wording, each attributed. The limits are stated:
  Cochrane rated all main outcomes very low quality (design flaws, small samples) and found insufficient evidence for a best form of
  exercise; the 5–8-year follow-up had a 19.3% response. The guideline's recommendations on taping, braces, foot orthoses, manual therapy
  and biophysical agents are quoted as "the guideline states…". No advice, no plan, no sample week.
- **Medical-type post: a sports physiotherapist's or sports-medicine doctor's review of the wording, plus native-speaker review of the
  6 translations, is recommended before publication.**

## 20 Nov 2026: data-only rewrite of all 30 posts

Owner's rule: "No gray area. No suggestions. No decision on reader's behalf for every blog. We only give data to consume so people can draw their own conclusions instead of giving our opinion."

- All 30 posts × 7 languages rewritten to `"format": "evidence"`, `modified: 2026-11-20`. Each line is an attributed finding from a numbered source. Removed: advice, imperatives, coach voice, sample weeks and plans, "best"/"7 moves" rankings, and in-article CTAs. Safety lines now read "guideline X states…". Slugs unchanged; JSON-LD (headline, description, dateModified) follows the new copy.
- Exercise lists are limited to exercises used in the cited studies or set by the cited rulebook. Figures were re-checked against the abstracts, rulebook PDFs and organiser pages. Figures that changed since the original posts were updated to the current page, and claims that could not be found in a source were dropped (list below).

- `verify.mjs`: new English advice-phrase check on English post articles and the fat-loss page. `tools/blog/README.md`: new "Editorial rule: data only" section.

- Not done: native-speaker review of the 6 translations, and medical review of the health posts' wording. Both are recommended before publication. The 6 zip-only posts are not part of this generator and were not rewritten; some of their titles still appear in "More from the blog" link text.

## 19 Nov 2026: batch 30

- New post: **Best strength exercises for cyclists: 7 moves and a sample week**
  (`/blog/best-strength-exercises-for-cyclists`) in en, ar (RTL), hi, es, zh, ja, gu, with hreflang (7 + x-default),
  cards in all 7 blog indexes, 7 sitemap URLs, and a link in the fat-loss page's "Read next". Data: `tools/blog/data/posts/best-strength-exercises-for-cyclists.json`, top of `order.json`.
  - Audience: road and indoor cyclists (a new sport audience for the blog, next to the runners post). See also: runners post; core and lower back post.
  - Moves: goblet squat then back squat (barbell only later, with coaching), Romanian deadlift, rear-foot-elevated split squat (regression:
    back foot on the floor), step-up (lower step if the knee grumbles), one-arm row, side plank (bent knees first), calf raises then small hops.
  - Sources (10, all fetched and checked against the PubMed abstract or the publisher page): WHO 2020 guidelines; Rønnestad & Mujika 2014 review;
    Beattie 2014 systematic review (26 studies); Yamamoto 2010 systematic review (5 RCTs in highly trained road cyclists); Rønnestad, Hansen &
    Raastad 2011 (5-min all-out after 185 min, 371 to 400 W); Vikmoen 2016 (19 female cyclists, 11 weeks); Mujika, Rønnestad & Martin 2016
    (in-season maintenance); Clarsen 2010 (overuse injuries in 109 professional road cyclists: 58% low back pain, 36% anterior knee pain);
    Olmedillas 2012 (cycling and bone, 31 studies); Lauersen 2018 (strength training and sports injuries, RR 0.338, not cyclist-specific).
  - Honest framing: small groups of well-trained riders, not a promise; 2 of 5 RCTs found no gain when lifting was stacked on top of the
    usual riding (so the post says swap, don't stack); the injury meta-analysis is not in cyclists; the trials' 4–10 RM loads are not the start.
  - Physio-safety: 1 to 3 reps in reserve, never to failure; traffic-light pain check (labelled a coaching rule of thumb; anterior knee pain
    that builds on the bike, back pain spreading down a leg, or pain that changes movement is always a stop); impact capped at 3 × 10 small
    hops, no drop jumps, hops left out with heel, Achilles or shin pain or diagnosed low bone density; sample week labelled as a sample with a
    load cap; see a doctor or physio first with a heart condition, high blood pressure, diabetes, a recent crash, fracture or concussion, or
    low bone density. Coached section: Iron Hour 60 min 1:1 with a minute-by-minute outline; Lean Reset, Return, Open Floor, 30-minute
    consultation (not training); no prices (booking holds a time, fee agreed directly). ASCA Level 1 and ACE named without implying endorsement.
- Header fixes on every generated page (210 posts, 7 blog indexes, fat-loss page), via `localizeHeader` in `build.mjs`:
  - The logo link's `aria-label` said the brand twice ("Online Fitness Worldwide, Online Fitness Worldwide: Home"); it now says
    "Online Fitness Worldwide: <Home in the page language>".
  - The header "Programs" link was English on non-English pages; it now uses `PROGRAMS_LABEL` (new in `data/shared.json`: البرامج, प्रोग्राम,
    Programas, 训练项目, プログラム, પ્રોગ્રામ), with `hreflang="en"` because it still points at the English home section (`/#programs`;
    the site has no language homes). On the 203 existing post pages this changes only those two strings (checked by script: nothing else differs).
  - `verify.mjs` now also fails on a repeated brand in the home-link label, an English "Programs" link on a non-English page, or a missing
    Programs link (tested: an old page fails both checks).
- README counts updated (30 new posts, 210 pages, 261 sitemap URLs), `localizeHeader` / `PROGRAMS_LABEL` and the new checks noted, plus a cyclists section.
- Verify: OK, 218 pages, 261 sitemap URLs (was 211 / 254). No old brand name or old location in the output.
- Pushed in 58 connector commits (max 4 files each): 5 for the post, indexes, sitemap, fat-loss page, generator and labels; 51 for the header

## 18 Nov 2026: batch 29

- New post: **Best strength exercises for teenagers: 7 moves and a sample week**
  (`/blog/best-strength-exercises-for-teenagers`) in en, ar (RTL), hi, es, zh, ja, gu, with hreflang (7 + x-default),
  cards in all 7 blog indexes, 7 sitemap URLs, and a link in the fat-loss page's "Read next". Data: `tools/blog/data/posts/best-strength-exercises-for-teenagers.json`, top of `order.json`.
  - Audience: teenagers and their parents (a new audience for the blog; ties to the "online strength and conditioning coach" and
    "personal trainer at home" queries). See also: strength and conditioning for beginners; warm-up post.
  - Moves: goblet squat, hip hinge then Romanian deadlift (broomstick first), push-up (counter, then floor), one-arm row, reverse lunge,
    dead bug, pogo hops and soft landings (5 landings a set, no box jumps or drop jumps).
  - Honest framing: the health findings (Smith 2014) are associations, not proof that lifting causes them; links with pain and cognition were
    inconsistent or uncertain; the knee-injury estimate in Emery 2015 was not statistically significant; the heavy, low-rep doses from
    youth-athlete studies (Lesinski 2016) are for high skill and in-person coaching, not home training.
  - Safety: under 18, a parent or guardian knows and agrees and is there for set-up; NHS: medical advice with a health condition, start slowly,
    weights only when a qualified adult trains and supervises; AAP: see a doctor first with uncontrolled hypertension or seizures, certain heart
    conditions or previous anthracycline chemotherapy, a paediatric cardiologist with complex congenital heart disease, no resistance training
    with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, pulmonary hypertension or Marfan syndrome, any sign of illness, injury or overuse checked before training
    resumes; most reported lifting injuries happen on home equipment, unsupervised (so no heavy lifting alone, no max attempts, collars on);
    eating, weight or body-image worries go to a doctor; no diets or weight cutting; performance-enhancing substances a hard no; chest pain,
    fainting, a racing heart or disproportionate breathlessness: stop and get medical help. 1 to 3 reps in reserve, traffic-light pain check
    (labelled a coaching rule of thumb; joint, heel or below-kneecap pain is never amber), small, few jumps (AAP: explosive efforts can stress
    tendon-attachment growth areas before skeletal maturity), 1–2 days a week off sport, total training load counted. Sample week (healthy
    teenager, no condition needing a doctor's input, no current injury, parent on board): two ~30-minute sessions, 1–2 sets, bodyweight then
    light loads for 12 clean reps, no max lifts or tests, no failure, no drop jumps, no timed workouts. Coach, not doctor or physio.
  - Evidence as stated by the sources: WHO 2020 (5–17: average ≥60 min/day moderate-to-vigorous, mostly aerobic; vigorous and muscle- and
    bone-strengthening activities ≥3 days/week); NHS (same for 5–18; weights if a qualified adult trains and supervises); Stricker 2020 AAP
    (strength gains with low injury rates when technique-focused and well supervised; no shown harm to growth plates, height or the heart;
    pre-puberty gains mainly neural; ≥8 weeks at 2–3×/week, >23 weeks most effective; start 1–2 sets of 8–12 at ≤60% 1RM, add 5–10%, later
    2–4 sets of 6–12 at ≤80% 1RM; ~1 min rest for beginners; sessions ≥20–30 min on non-consecutive days; 1–2 days off sport a week; medical
    conditions list; home-equipment injuries; bodybuilding screening); Faigenbaum 2009 NSCA (many adult benefits attainable with age-specific
    guidelines); Lloyd 2014 (international consensus adapted from the UKSCA statement, endorsed by sports medicine, exercise science and
    paediatrics organisations); Faigenbaum and Myer 2010 (most reported injuries in young lifters accidental; lack of qualified instruction,
    poor technique and inappropriate loads may partly explain some); Behringer 2010 (overall effect size 1.12 on strength; trainability rises with
    age and maturity, no noticeable boost at puberty; duration and sets positive); Lesinski 2016 (43 studies, youth athletes 6–18: moderate
    effects on strength and jump, small on sprint, agility and sport-specific performance); Smith 2014 (110 studies: strong evidence linking
    muscular fitness with lower total and central adiposity, CVD and metabolic risk factors, better bone health and self-esteem; pain and
    cognition inconsistent or uncertain; associations generally low to moderate); Emery 2015 (25 studies; RCT meta-analysis: lower-limb injury
    IRR 0.64; knee IRR 0.74, not significant).
  - Sources (10): WHO 2020; NHS "Physical activity guidelines for children and young people" (reviewed 23 Oct 2024); Stricker 2020 Pediatrics
    (AAP, reaffirmed Nov 2024); Faigenbaum 2009 J Strength Cond Res (NSCA); Lloyd 2014 Br J Sports Med; Faigenbaum and Myer 2010 Br J Sports Med;
    Behringer 2010 Pediatrics; Lesinski 2016 Br J Sports Med; Smith 2014 Sports Med; Emery 2015 Br J Sports Med. DOIs checked with Crossref,
    URLs with curl; figures checked against the PubMed abstracts, the AAP full text and the NHS page on 18 Nov 2026 (Lloyd 2014 and the NSCA
    statement are cited only for what their abstracts say).
- SEO/accessibility fix (open item from batches 27 and 28): the footer "Read the blog" button was English on every page and linked to the
  English `/blog`. It is now in the page language and links to that language's blog index (`FOOTER_BLOG_LABEL` in `data/shared.json`;
  ar "اقرأ المدونة", hi "ब्लॉग पढ़ें", es "Lee el blog", zh "阅读博客", ja "ブログを読む", gu "બ્લોગ વાંચો"), applied by `build.mjs`
  (`localizeFooterBlogLink`) to every generated non-English post page and the 6 non-English blog indexes. On the 168 existing non-English post
  pages this changes only that link and label (checked with a word diff: one href and one label per page, nothing else). The 6 posts that live
  only in the zip keep the zip's footer.
- `verify.mjs` now also fails on an English footer blog button (or one pointing at `/blog`) on a non-English page, a `<title>` over 78
  characters and a meta description over 160 (all pages pass).
- README counts updated (29 new posts, 203 pages, 254 sitemap URLs), `FOOTER_BLOG_LABEL` and the new verify checks noted, plus a teenagers section.
- Verify: OK, 211 pages, 254 sitemap URLs (was 204 / 247). No old brand name or old location in the output (verify check + grep: 0).
- Pushed in 49 connector commits (max 4 files each): 5 for the post, indexes, sitemap, fat-loss page and generator, 42 for the footer button on

## 17 Nov 2026: batch 28

- New post: **Best strength exercises for cancer survivors: 7 moves and a sample week**
  (`/blog/best-strength-exercises-for-cancer-survivors`) in en, ar (RTL), hi, es, zh, ja, gu, with hreflang (7 + x-default),
  cards in all 7 blog indexes, 7 sitemap URLs, and a link in the fat-loss page's "Read next". Data: `tools/blog/data/posts/best-strength-exercises-for-cancer-survivors.json`, top of `order.json`.
  - Moves: sit-to-stand, wall push-up, one-arm row, glute bridge, step-up (holding the rail), calf raise at the counter, light farmer's carry.
  - Honest framing: strength work does not treat cancer; it helps people keep doing what their day needs. The Cochrane review (56 trials, 4,826 people,
    during treatment) found exercise may improve quality of life, physical function and fatigue, and its authors ask for cautious reading
    (very different programmes, many trials at high risk of bias); effects on chemo nerve damage and cognition are uncertain (ACSM roundtable);
    WHO's lower cancer and all-cause mortality with activity after diagnosis (breast and colorectal cancer survivors) is described as a link across
    many people, not a promise for one.
  - Safety: talk to the cancer doctor or specialist nurse first; the roundtable's NCCN-adapted triage (no other problems: no extra check; neuropathy,
    arthritis, weak bones or lymphoedema: medical check first; lung/abdominal surgery, stoma, heart or lung disease, poor coordination, extreme tiredness,
    severe nutrition problems, worsening condition or bone metastases: medical evaluation and clearance first); ACSM screening line; Macmillan's
    precautions (don't exercise when unwell; low white cells: avoid busy gyms and pools if advised; low platelets: gentle, no high impact; central/PICC
    line: no swimming, no vigorous upper-body work) and stop-and-see-a-doctor symptoms; fever or infection signs during treatment: call the hospital's
    emergency contact, not the coach. 1 to 3 reps in reserve, traffic-light check for pain and tiredness (labelled a coaching rule of thumb), new or
    unusual bone, back or muscle pain is never amber. Sample week (for someone cleared by their team, no bone metastases, no new symptoms, no lymphoedema
    flare; strength days away from chemo days) with no impact, no maximal lifts, no failure, no breath-holding, no sit-ups, light loads, a set before weight.
    Lymphoedema: compression garment if prescribed, lighter on that side, smaller steps; stoma/tummy surgery: ask the surgeon or stoma nurse, low load;
    numb feet or unsteady: skip step-ups; numb hands: lifting gloves; bone metastases: specialist plan only. Coach not doctor, nurse or physio.
  - Evidence as stated by the sources: WHO 2020 (chronic conditions incl. cancer: activity considered safe without contraindication; 150–300 min/week
    moderate aerobic plus muscle-strengthening on 2+ days); Macmillan (usually safe before, during or after treatment; 2–3 days of soreness common;
    bone problems: avoid high impact; neuropathy: care with steps, gloves; lymphoedema: compression garment, build up slowly); Campbell 2019 ACSM roundtable
    ("avoid inactivity"; aerobic ≥3×/week ≥30 min for 8–12 weeks; adding resistance ≥2×/week, ≥2 sets of 8–15 at ≥60% 1RM gives similar benefits;
    supervised start-low, progress-slow resistance training safe with lymphoedema, unsupervised not yet known; bone-health trials used about a year of
    supervised resistance plus impact); Ligibel 2022 ASCO (oncology providers should recommend aerobic and resistance exercise during curative-intent
    treatment; improved fitness, strength, fatigue); Cormie 2018 COSA (exercise part of standard care; towards 150 min aerobic and 2–3 resistance
    sessions/week; refer to an exercise physiologist or physio with cancer experience); Schmitz 2009 NEJM (141 survivors with stable arm lymphoedema;
    twice-weekly slowly progressive lifting with compression garment; no increase in swelling, exacerbations 14% vs 29%, better symptoms and strength);
    Campbell 2022 bone metastases consensus (plan with the health care team, deliver by exercise professionals with oncology training; posture,
    controlled movement, technique).
  - Sources (10): WHO 2020; Macmillan "Exercising safely when you have cancer" (reviewed 1 Nov 2023); Campbell 2019 Med Sci Sports Exerc;
    Ligibel 2022 J Clin Oncol (ASCO); Cormie 2018 Med J Aust (COSA); Mishra 2012 Cochrane CD008465.pub2; Schmitz 2009 N Engl J Med;
    Campbell 2022 JCO Oncol Pract; ACSM 2009 progression; Riebe 2015 ACSM screening. DOIs checked with Crossref, URLs with curl; figures checked
    against the PubMed abstracts, the Campbell 2019 and WHO 2020 full texts (PMC) and the Macmillan page on 17 Nov 2026.
  - See also: mood, stress and sleep post; bone density post.

## 16 Nov 2026: batch 27

- New post: **Best strength exercises for postpartum recovery: 7 moves and a sample week**
  (`/blog/best-strength-exercises-for-postpartum-recovery`) in en, ar (RTL), hi, es, zh, ja, gu, with hreflang (7 + x-default),
  cards in all 7 blog indexes, 7 sitemap URLs, and a link in the fat-loss page's "Read next". Data: `tools/blog/data/posts/best-strength-exercises-for-postpartum-recovery.json`, top of `order.json`.
  - Moves: pelvic floor squeeze, side-lying tummy draw-in (the NHS's own starter exercise), glute bridge, goblet squat to a chair, one-arm row,
    incline push-up, light dumbbell Romanian deadlift.
  - Honest framing: when to start depends on how you gave birth and any complications (pelvic floor exercises can start straight away);
    the Cochrane review found the clearest benefit for pelvic floor training started early in pregnancy, and the effect of training started after
    birth on later leaking was uncertain ("we do it, and we don't oversell it"); a tummy gap is common, and the evidence that exercise closes it is
    mixed (ACOG cites studies that reduced it; a 175-woman RCT did not change prevalence at 6 or 12 months), so the tummy is trained for everyday
    strength, not a number; the return-to-running guideline is based on evidence and clinical experience, not a rule for every body.
  - Safety: 1 to 3 reps in reserve, traffic-light pain check with leaking, heaviness/dragging in the vagina or tummy doming counted as amber (labelled
    a coaching rule of thumb), no running, jumping or skipping for at least the first 3 months, sample week with no impact, no sit-ups, no front planks yet,
    no maximal lifts, no failure, no breath-holding (a coaching choice), light loads (start no heavier than the baby in the car seat, from the guideline),
    regressions on every move, sample week for someone at least 6 to 8 weeks after an uncomplicated birth with her doctor's OK, "check with your doctor or
    midwife before you start" after a caesarean, bad tear, heavy bleeding, high blood pressure in pregnancy or other complications, NHS "ask a GP or health
    visitor" and 8-week tummy-gap lines, NICE supervised pelvic floor training referral for leaking/heaviness/bulge, low mood: tell the doctor or health
    visitor now, red flags (heavy bleeding, chest pain, painful swollen leg, unusual breathlessness, dizziness), breastfeeding note from ACOG,
    coach not doctor, midwife or physio. Arabic addresses the reader in the feminine.
  - Evidence as stated by the sources: WHO 2020 (postpartum women without contraindication: regular activity, at least 150 min/week moderate aerobic,
    aerobic and muscle-strengthening activities, gentle stretching may help; daily pelvic floor training may reduce the risk of urinary incontinence);
    NHS post-pregnancy body (postnatal check at 6 to 8 weeks; gap usually back to normal by 8 weeks, see a GP if still obvious; with a gap, avoid sit-ups,
    planks and high-impact exercise to start with, straining and heavy lifting; pelvic floor dose: up to 10 long squeezes of up to 10 s and 10 quick,
    at least 3 times a day; side-lying tummy exercise, 10 s holds, up to 10 times); ACOG CO 804 (resume gradually as soon as medically safe, depending on
    delivery mode and complications; pelvic floor exercises in the immediate postpartum period; aerobic exercise doesn't affect milk production,
    composition or infant growth; feed or express first, hydrate); NICE NG210 (encourage pelvic floor training after birth; supervised programme of at
    least 3 months for stress or mixed urinary incontinence; 3-month supervised programme after assisted birth, OP baby or anal sphincter injury);
    Woodley 2020 Cochrane (46 trials, 10,832 women); Sperstad 2016 (300 first-time mothers; gap ≥2 finger widths in 60% at 6 weeks, 45.4% at 6 months,
    32.6% at 12 months; no difference in lumbopelvic pain at 12 months); Gluppe 2018 (175 primiparous women, 4-month programme from 6 weeks postpartum;
    RR 0.99 at 6 months, 1.04 at 12 months); Goom, Donnelly & Brockwell 2019 (low impact in the first 3 months; squats, lunges, bridging from weeks 2–4;
    light deadlifts from weeks 6–8, no more than the baby in a car seat; running 3–6 months at the earliest, after load and impact tests).
  - Sources (10): WHO 2020; NHS "Your post-pregnancy body", reviewed 20 July 2026; ACOG Committee Opinion 804 (Obstet Gynecol 2020, reaffirmed 2023);
    NICE NG210 (2021); Woodley 2020 Cochrane CD007471.pub4; Sperstad 2016 Br J Sports Med; Gluppe 2018 Phys Ther; Goom, Donnelly & Brockwell 2019
    returning to running postnatal guideline (PDF); ACSM 2009 progression; Riebe 2015 ACSM screening. DOIs checked with Crossref, URLs with curl;
    figures checked against the PubMed abstracts, the WHO 2020 BJSM full text (Europe PMC), the NHS and NICE pages, the ACOG text and the guideline PDF on 16 Nov 2026.
  - See also: busy parents post, mood, stress and sleep post.

## 15 Nov 2026: batch 26

- New post: **Best strength exercises for fatty liver (MASLD): 7 moves and a sample week**
  (`/blog/best-strength-exercises-for-fatty-liver`) in en, ar (RTL), hi, es, zh, ja, gu, with hreflang (7 + x-default),
  cards in all 7 blog indexes, 7 sitemap URLs, and a link in the fat-loss page's "Read next". Data: `tools/blog/data/posts/best-strength-exercises-for-fatty-liver.json`, top of `order.json`.
  - Moves: goblet squat, dumbbell Romanian deadlift, incline push-up, one-arm dumbbell row, glute bridge, half-kneeling one-arm overhead press, farmer's carry.
  - Name: the copy explains that non-alcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD) is now called metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD);
    the NHS page still uses NAFLD with MASLD as "also known as". Titles say "fatty liver (MASLD)" (what people search).
  - Honest framing: exercise lowers liver fat even with little or no weight loss, but did not change ALT; the strength-specific studies are small
    ("a good signal, not the whole answer"); the guideline says evidence for effects on fibrosis and long-term liver outcomes is less robust than for
    cardiometabolic benefits; weight loss (mostly through food) helps the liver most when overweight; normal-weight MASLD still gets diet and exercise.
  - Safety: 1 to 3 reps in reserve, traffic-light pain check (labelled a coaching rule of thumb), load cap (no maximal lifts, no failure, no breath-holding,
    no jumping, no running; labelled a coaching choice), strength on non-consecutive days (Mon/Wed/Fri), regressions on every move, "check with your doctor
    before you start", NHS "see a GP" lines, yearly check-ups and liver specialist for fibrosis/cirrhosis, guideline alcohol advice (discouraged; stop
    completely with advanced fibrosis or cirrhosis), cirrhosis / swollen tummy or legs / varices: get the liver team's OK first and don't hold your breath,
    coach not doctor or dietitian (liver tests, scans, medicines, medical diets stay with the doctor or dietitian).
  - Evidence as stated by the sources: EASL–EASD–EASO 2024 (physical activity to reduce steatosis, preferably >150 min/week moderate or 75 min/week vigorous;
    with overweight, weight loss ≥5% for liver fat, 7–10% for inflammation, ≥10% for fibrosis; less robust evidence for histology/liver outcomes;
    normal-weight MASLD: diet and exercise to reduce liver fat; alcohol discouraged, stopped with advanced fibrosis/cirrhosis); Keating 2012 (12 controlled
    trials; exercise lowered liver fat with minimal or no weight loss; no effect on ALT); Stine 2023 (14 RCTs, 551 people; OR 3.51 for ≥30% relative MRI
    liver-fat reduction; ≥750 MET-min/week, e.g. 150 min/week brisk walking, needed; independent of >5% weight loss); Hashida 2017 (aerobic and resistance
    both reduced steatosis; resistance at lower intensity/energy; median effective resistance protocol 45 min, 3x/week, 12 weeks); Hallsworth 2011
    (8 weeks resistance, n=11 vs 8 controls; 13% relative fall in liver lipid; no change in body weight); Zelber-Sagi 2014 (RCT, 82 randomised, 64 per protocol;
    3 months resistance training 3x/week vs home stretching; larger fall in ultrasound liver-fat index, less trunk fat, more lean mass).
  - Sources (10): WHO 2020; NHS NAFLD (also known as MASLD), reviewed 31 July 2025; EASL–EASD–EASO 2024 MASLD guideline executive summary (Diabetologia);
    Keating 2012 J Hepatol; Stine 2023 Am J Gastroenterol; Hashida 2017 J Hepatol; Hallsworth 2011 Gut; Zelber-Sagi 2014 World J Gastroenterol;
    ACSM 2009 progression; Riebe 2015 ACSM screening. URLs/DOIs checked with curl (doi.org and Crossref); figures checked against the PubMed abstracts,
    the guideline full text (Europe PMC) and the NHS page on 15 Nov 2026. NICE NG49 (now titled MASLD) has no exercise dose and is not cited.
  - See also: type 2 diabetes post, fat loss post.

  - Moves: goblet squat to a chair, dumbbell Romanian deadlift, incline push-up, one-arm dumbbell row, step-up, farmer's carry, glute bridge.

    with both names in use during the transition; the NHS page now uses PMOS. Titles keep "PCOS" (what people search) with "(PMOS)".
  - Honest framing: lifting does not cure PCOS; exercise gives modest, low-certainty improvements (insulin, insulin resistance, lipids, waist, fitness);
    lifestyle may cut weight and free androgen index; no trial measured live birth or period regularity, so exercise is not fertility treatment;
    the only resistance-specific study is single-arm (labelled "a promising signal, not proof"). Weight stigma: the scale is one number, not the only one.
  - Safety: 1 to 3 reps in reserve, traffic-light pain check (labelled a coaching rule of thumb), load cap (no maximal lifts, no failure, no jumping,
    no running; labelled a coaching choice), strength on non-consecutive days, regressions on every move, "check with your doctor before you start",
    NHS "see a GP" lines and raised risk of type 2 diabetes, high BP and heart disease, pregnancy/fertility treatment: ask your doctor,
    coach not doctor or dietitian (metformin, the pill, fertility care stay with the doctor). Arabic addresses the reader in the feminine.
  - Evidence as stated by the sources: Kite 2019 (18 trials; vs control lower fasting insulin, HOMA-IR, total/LDL cholesterol, triglycerides;
    vs usual care better VO2max, waist -2.62 cm, body fat -1.39%; low to very low quality); Cochrane Lim 2019 (15 RCTs, 498 women; weight -1.68 kg,
    FAI -1.11; low quality; no live birth/miscarriage/menstrual regularity data); Kogure 2016 (45 sedentary women with PCOS, PRT 3x/week for 4 months;
    testosterone and waist down, lean mass index up; non-randomised, single arm); ESSA 2024 (150-300 min moderate or 75-150 vigorous plus strength on
    2 non-consecutive days; at least 250/150 min for modest weight loss; weight stigma); Teede 2023 guideline (lifestyle emphasis; low to moderate quality evidence).
  - Sources (10): WHO 2020; NHS PMOS (previously PCOS), reviewed 30 June 2026; Teede 2023 international PCOS guideline (Fertil Steril);
    Teede 2026 Lancet name-change consensus; Cochrane, Lim 2019 (CD007506); Kite 2019 Syst Rev; Sabag 2024 ESSA position statement (J Sci Med Sport);
    Kogure 2016 MSSE; ACSM 2009 progression; Riebe 2015 ACSM screening. URLs/DOIs checked with curl (Cochrane DOI via Crossref);
    figures checked against the PubMed abstracts and the NHS page on 14 Nov 2026. NICE CKS on PCOS is UK-only (blocked) and is not cited.

- New post: **Best strength exercises for fat loss: 7 moves and a sample week**
  (`/blog/best-strength-exercises-for-fat-loss`) in en, ar (RTL), hi, es, zh, ja, gu, with hreflang (7 + x-default),
  cards in all 7 blog indexes, 7 sitemap URLs, and a link in the fat-loss page's "Read next". Data: `tools/blog/data/posts/best-strength-exercises-for-fat-loss.json`, top of `order.json`.
  - Moves: goblet squat (chair sit-to-stand regression), dumbbell Romanian deadlift, incline push-up, one-arm dumbbell row, split squat, farmer's carry, dead bug.
  - Honest framing: fat loss needs an energy deficit (NHS, NICE NG246); strength training alone gives small fat loss; walking/aerobic work cuts more body weight
    and fat; lifting keeps or adds lean mass. Exercise helps health even when the scale doesn't move (Cochrane).
  - Safety: 1 to 3 reps in reserve, traffic-light pain check (0-2 green, 3-5 amber, above 5 or worse next morning red; labelled a coaching rule of thumb),
    load cap (no maximal lifts, no failure, no jumping, no running; labelled a coaching choice), regressions on every move, "check with your doctor before you start"
    if you have a condition or take regular medicines, NHS "see a GP" list (BMI range plus wanting help, waist over half height, diabetes, high BP, joint pain,
    sleep apnoea, eating disorder), weight-loss medicines still need diet and activity (NHS), coach not doctor or dietitian.
  - Evidence as stated by the sources: Wewege 2022 (58 trials; -1.46 points body fat %, -0.55 kg fat mass, lower visceral fat vs no exercise);
    STRRIDE AT/RT, Willis 2012 (119 adults, 8 months: aerobic and combined cut more weight and fat than resistance; resistance and combined added more lean mass);
    Villareal 2017 NEJM (160 older adults with obesity: ~9% weight loss in all exercise groups; lean mass -2 to -3% with resistance/combined vs -5% aerobic only);
    Cochrane Shaw 2006 (43 trials: exercise plus diet about 1.1 kg more than diet alone); ACSM Donnelly 2009 (150-250 min/week modest, >250 min/week clinically significant;
    resistance training does not add scale weight loss); NICE NG246 (45-60 min/day to prevent obesity, 60-90 min/day after weight loss); NHS 0.5-1 kg a week, 5-10% helps.
  - Sources (10): WHO 2020; NHS Overweight and obesity in adults; NICE NG246 (physical activity and diet); Cochrane, Shaw 2006 (CD003817);
    Wewege 2022 Sports Med; Willis 2012 J Appl Physiol; Donnelly 2009 ACSM position stand; Villareal 2017 NEJM; ACSM 2009 progression; Riebe 2015 ACSM screening.
    All URLs/DOIs checked with curl; figures checked against the PubMed abstracts and the NHS/NICE pages on 13 Nov 2026.
  - See also: one-dumbbell home workouts post, type 2 diabetes post.
`````

`````
From OnlineFitnessWorldwide Google Books research (KB entry 16: /workspace/research/knowledge-base/2026-11-20.md). Brand: Online Fitness Worldwide only. Worldwide audience; never India-first. ## 2026-11-20/coaching-standards/review-outdoor-starter-pack-v1.md
`````
`````
# Coaching Standards review: Outdoor Starter Pack v1 (selected sections)

**DRAFT review for Susmit. Nothing has been sent to anyone, and the outdoor file has not been edited.**
**Reviewer:** Coaching Standards (OnlineFitnessWorldwide)
**Date:** Friday 20 November 2026 (Asia/Calcutta, IST)

**File reviewed:** `/workspace/outdoor-adventure-coach/outdoor-starter-pack-v1.md`

**Sections reviewed:**
- 2D Acclimatisation education (lines 256–274)
- Finger-loading education in section 3 (lines 300–315)
- 5 Water safety (lines 367–371)
- 6 "What we don't do" safety page (lines 388–418)
- **Extra:** 1F Clinician-first screen (lines 82–90)

**Also checked, only where they bear on these sections:** the section 7 hand-off map (lines 422–439), the section 8 sources (lines 443–469), and line 106 (18+ rule).

**Standard used:** `/workspace/coaching-standards/coaching-standards-v1.md` (DRAFT v1). The relevant parts are §2 scope-of-practice map, §4 claims checklist, §5 safeguarding and §6 stop-and-seek-help signs.

**Context read:** `/workspace/outdoor-adventure-coach/FIRST_JOB.md`, `READING_LIST_2026-11-19.md` and KB entry 15 (`/workspace/research/knowledge-base/2026-11-19.md`).

> This review is a standards check. It is not medical or legal advice. The suggested wording is for Outdoor Adventure Coach to adapt. Public bodies are cited as sources only; none of them endorses OnlineFitnessWorldwide.

---

## Verdicts

| Section | Verdict | One-line reason |
|---|---|---|
| **Overall** | **Ready with fixes. Not for clients until the must-fix items are done.** | Scope discipline, brand and framing are strong. There are three gaps: no altitude warning signs, "Physio" is presented as if it were a clinician, and the "what we don't do" page is missing a few lines. |
| 2D Acclimatisation education | Ready with fixes (1 must-fix) | Correctly avoids ascent rates, medication and treatment. But it gives no warning signs and no "don't go higher / go down if worse" rule. |
| Finger-loading education | Ready with fixes (1 must-fix) | Conservative, with clear pain rules. But "Physio decides when, and how, you load it again" gives our Physio teammate bot a clinician's job. |
| 5 Water safety | Ready with small fixes (should-fix only) | Correctly refers clients out. A few sourced basics would help: never go alone, lifeguarded beaches, cold-water note for paddlers. |
| 6 "What we don't do" | Ready with fixes (1 must-fix, plus the Physio wording) | Says "guide, lead, supervise". Doesn't say "certify / assess readiness", "not a substitute for local guides, instructors or lifeguards", or what to do in an emergency. |
| Extra: 1F Clinician-first screen | Ready with small fixes (should-fix only) | Conservative and matches CDC guidance. The hand-off line sends lung conditions and altitude history to Metabolic Health, which is outside its scope. |

---

## Must-fix (before any client use)

### M1. 2D (lines 258–272): no altitude warning signs and no "don't go higher / go down if worse" rule

Current wording (line 261):
> "We don't advise on ascent rates, rest days, symptoms, medication, oxygen or what to do if you feel unwell."

**Why it matters**

Keeping ascent rates, medication and treatment out is right. Nothing here names a drug, a dose or an ascent figure. That passes.

The problem is that the page gives a high-altitude client **no** stop-and-seek-help signs at all. Coaching Standards §6 requires stop-and-seek-help signs for safety topics. Public health sources agree on a short, non-medical core message:
- don't go higher with symptoms;
- go down if symptoms get worse;
- some signs are an emergency.

**What the public sources say (checked 20 November 2026, IST)**

- **NHS, Altitude sickness**
  - Main symptoms: headache, loss of appetite, feeling or being sick, tiredness, dizziness, difficulty sleeping.
  - If unwell: tell someone you're travelling with, and do not climb higher.
  - If symptoms get worse or don't improve after 1 day: go lower.
  - Get medical help immediately for: confusion; problems with balance or coordination; hallucinations; breathlessness even at rest; a cough or frothy or bloody spit; blue or grey skin, lips, tongue or nails; being very sleepy or hard to wake.
- **CDC, Travel to High Altitudes**
  - People with altitude illness should not go higher until symptoms have gone.
  - If symptoms get worse while resting, go lower.
  - HACE (brain swelling: extreme fatigue, drowsiness, confusion, loss of coordination) and HAPE (fluid on the lungs: shortness of breath, weakness, cough) mean the person must immediately move, or be moved, lower.
- **CDC Yellow Book 2026, High-Altitude Travel and Altitude Illness** (clinician chapter)
  - If symptoms worsen at the same altitude despite treatment, descent is mandatory.
  - For HAPE, descent is urgent.
  - Ataxia (loss of coordination) and confusion signal HACE.

**Suggested addition to 2D**

Add a short box before "Questions to take to your appointment". It gives no doses, no drug names and no ascent numbers.

> **Know the warning signs (awareness only, not medical advice)**
> - Common early signs at altitude: headache, feeling sick, loss of appetite, tiredness, dizziness, poor sleep (NHS; CDC).
> - **Feeling unwell at altitude? Tell your guide or group, and don't go any higher** until you feel better (NHS; CDC).
> - **Getting worse, or not improving? Go down**, with your guide's help (NHS; CDC).
> - **Emergency, go down now and get help:** confusion, clumsiness or trouble walking straight, very sleepy or hard to wake, breathless even at rest, a cough with frothy or bloody spit, or blue or grey lips or nails (NHS; CDC).
> - Your doctor or travel or mountain-medicine clinic, and your guide, decide what to do. We don't give treatment, medication or ascent advice.

**Keep out**
- NHS and CDC numbers: 2,500 m, 300–1,000 m lower, 500 m per night, 2,750 m.
- Any medicine names, including NHS's ibuprofen and paracetamol line and the Yellow Book's prescription drugs.

These belong to the clinician and the operator's itinerary, as line 261 already says. Add NHS, CDC and the CDC Yellow Book to section 8 with "source only, no endorsement".

*If Susmit prefers 2D to give no symptom list at all, the minimum is one line:* "If you feel unwell at altitude, tell your guide, don't go higher, and go down if you're getting worse (NHS; CDC)." (See question 1.)

### M2. Finger-loading (line 313), section 6 (line 411) and 1G (line 93): "Physio" reads as a clinician and is given a clinician's decision

Current wording:
> Line 313: "**Pain → see Physio.** Physio decides when, and how, you load it again."
> Line 411: "**Physio** (through us) for pain or injury, then a clinician as needed."

**Why it matters**

Physio is a teammate **bot**. In the KB index it is listed for "open-access injury-prevention notes and safety reviews". Coaching Standards §2 says bots draft and hand off, and that "clinical care stays with the client's physiotherapist or doctor."

In client-facing text, "see Physio" will be read as "see a physiotherapist". "Physio decides when you load it again" gives a bot a return-to-load decision, which is clinical care. This is both a scope issue and a misleading claim (claims checklist items 4 and 8).

**Suggested replacements**
- **Line 313:** "**Pain → stop and get it checked** by a physiotherapist or doctor. They decide when, and how, you load it again. We then adjust your plan to their advice (our Physio teammate reviews the plan changes)."
- **Line 411:** "**A physiotherapist or doctor** for pain or injury. Our team then fits your plan around their advice."
- **Line 305:** "only after a check with a physiotherapist or doctor if you have any finger, hand or elbow history."
- **Line 315:** "Elbow or shoulder pain that lingers more than a session or two: stop that loading and see a physiotherapist or doctor."

The same wording appears in 1G (line 93), and in lines 122, 152, 216 and 384, which are outside this review. Apply the same fix to any of them that become client-facing.

Section 7 is an internal map, so "Physio" can stay there. The column "keep pain-free work only if Physio agrees" should read "...only within the clinician's advice; Physio reviews the plan change."

If Susmit does work with a qualified human physiotherapist who is behind the "Physio" name, the wording can say so explicitly instead (question 2).

### M3. Section 6 (lines 392–413) doesn't say we don't certify or assess readiness, doesn't say we're not a substitute for local professionals, and has no emergency line

**What's there and passes**
- "Guide, lead, supervise or plan routes" (line 395).
- Technical-skill exclusions (lines 396–399).
- No altitude-medicine, medication or first-aid advice (line 400).
- No diagnosis or treatment (line 401).
- Under-18s (line 402).
- Refer-out list (lines 405–410).

**What's missing**
1. **We don't certify or sign off.** There is no line saying we don't assess, certify or sign off a client's skills, fitness or readiness for a trip or route. Without one, a client could take a good retest result or "you're ready" from a coach as a fitness-to-go clearance.
2. **Not a substitute.** There is no explicit "not a substitute for qualified local guides, instructors or lifeguards". "Learn those from" implies it, but doesn't say it.
3. **Emergencies.** There is no emergency line.

**Suggested additions**

Under "We don't":
> - Assess, certify or sign off your technical skills, or tell you that you're fit, safe or ready for a particular trip, route, climb or swell. That decision is yours, made with your guide, instructor, operator and doctor.

After "Learn those from":
> **Our training is not a substitute for qualified local guides, instructors, lifeguards or medical advice.** Conditions, rules and rescue services differ by country and by day. Follow local guidance.
> **In an emergency,** call the local emergency or mountain or sea rescue number for where you are. Numbers differ by country, so save them before you go. We can't help in real time from a video call.

**Wording fix:** Line 413 says "mountain days demand technical skills...". Extend it to "mountain, water and wall skills need hands-on teaching..." so that it covers surf, paddle and climbing too.

---

## Should-fix

**S1. Section 7, line 427 and 1F, line 83: altitude history and lung conditions are sent to Metabolic Health.**
Metabolic Health covers diabetes, blood pressure and cardiometabolic health. Altitude-illness history and lung conditions are outside its scope. CDC says people with heart or lung disease should talk to a doctor familiar with high-altitude medicine.

Suggested wording for line 83:
> "If any answer is 'yes' or 'not sure', we pause. You speak to your doctor (for altitude, ideally a travel or mountain-medicine clinic) first. Our team then routes the plan: Metabolic Health for diabetes, blood pressure or heart-health conditions; Women's Health for pregnancy or postpartum; Masters for clients roughly 40+; Physio for injury. Anything else goes to Susmit."

Change section 7, line 427 to: "Medical condition, medication → **client's clinician first**, then Metabolic Health if cardiometabolic. Altitude history → **client's doctor / travel or mountain-medicine clinic**."

**S2. Finger-loading, line 304: "roughly under a year" is an unsourced threshold.**
It is a sensible, conservative coaching rule. But the line 469 disclaimer only covers plan numbers, not safety thresholds. Either:
- label it ("Our starting rule, to be reviewed by Physio: new to climbing, roughly under a year…"); or
- attribute it once someone has read the source in Feehally or MacLeod. Only the catalogue pages were seen.

**S3. Finger-loading, line 314: make the urgency clear.**
Change it to: "A pop, sudden sharp pain, swelling or bruising in a finger: stop climbing **now** and get it checked by a doctor or physiotherapist **promptly**. Don't wait to see if it settles."

This is a conservative referral wording, not a diagnosis. No source is needed beyond the house rule, but label it as one.

**S4. Section 5, Water safety (lines 368–371): add three sourced basics.**
The section rightly refers out. Add these basics; they are awareness, not instruction:
> - Never surf or paddle alone. Choose lifeguarded beaches and stay between the flags where that system is used (RNLI).
> - Check local conditions and rules every time, and ask the lifeguards on the day. Rips, tides, wind, rocks, boats and marine life differ by place.
> - Paddlers: falling into cold water can make you gasp and lose control of your breathing. Ask your instructor about buoyancy aids and the right kit for the water temperature (RNLI, Cold water shock).

Also change "Book lessons through a recognised school" to "Learn with a recognised school". The bot never books, and the client should not read it as a booking service.

**S5. Section 8 (lines 443–469): sources need checkable links.**
Books are listed as author and title only. That's acceptable, but Coaching Standards §4 item 5 asks for real, checkable sources. Add the Google Books links from the reading list. I verified the ones relevant here today (see "Sources checked").

If M1 and S4 are adopted, add NHS, CDC, the CDC Yellow Book and RNLI under a "Public safety sources (source only, no endorsement)" heading.

---

## Nice-to-have

- **N1. 2D, line 262:** the gadget line matches KB entry 8, which says "most need careful, individual advice rather than gadgets". Fine as is. You could add "and don't use them instead of your clinician's advice".
- **N2. 2D:** under "Questions to take to your appointment", add "Will my medicines, or any condition, behave differently at altitude?" CDC notes this for diabetes, heart and lung disease, and pregnancy.
- **N3. Safeguarding.** Lines 106 and 402 already say 18+ only, which is good. Add one line to section 6: "Questions about a child or teenager (for example a school trek or junior surf camp) go to Susmit." Coaching Standards §5 requires this, and the section 7 hand-off map has no under-18 row. Add one: "Under-18 enquiries → **Susmit** (Coaching Standards flags)."
- **N4. 2D, line 274:** the source note is accurate to the KB. You could add that the book is a clinician-level reference and that the warning-sign box is from NHS and CDC, not from the book.

---

## Extra: 1F Clinician-first screen (short check)

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Conservative pause on "yes" or "not sure" | Pass |
| Covers heart, lung, blood pressure, blood sugar, medication, pregnancy, prior altitude illness, chest pain or fainting, clinician limits | Pass. This matches CDC's pre-existing-conditions list (heart, lung, diabetes, pregnancy). |
| Line 90: everyone going high sees a doctor or travel or mountain-medicine clinic | Pass. This is the right rule and matches CDC ("talk to a doctor before traveling to high elevation"). |
| No diagnosis, medication or dose | Pass |
| Hand-off routing | Should-fix (S1): lung conditions and altitude history are outside Metabolic Health's scope. Add Masters and Physio routing. |
| Clearance wording | Section 7 says "wait for clinician clearance". Fine for this conservative screen. Whether clearance is required, or "advice taken" is enough, is a question for Susmit (question 3). |

---

## Checks that passed (all reviewed sections)

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Brand | OnlineFitnessWorldwide only. A grep of the whole file found no retired brand name. |
| Prices | None. No currency symbols. |
| Endorsement | Clear disclaimers at line 15 and line 418, covering UIAA, The Mountaineers and mountain-medicine societies. |
| Altitude medication and ascent numbers | None in 2D. No drug names, doses or ascent rates anywhere in the reviewed sections. |
| Invented stats, results or stories | None. |
| Worldwide framing | Good. Kilimanjaro, Everest Base Camp, Inca Trail, Alps, Tasmania, Drakensberg, Fontainebleau, Joshua Tree, Rocklands, Ericeira, Bali, Taghazout, Byron Bay, Costa Rica and Jeffreys Bay. Not India-first. |
| Supervision | "Never guide or supervise" is reflected in section 6, line 395. Section 5, line 368 says "We train you on land. That's all." |
| KB claims | The 2D, finger-loading, water-safety and section 6 source notes match KB entry 15 and KB entry 8 takeaways. |

---

## Sources checked for this review (opened 20 November 2026, Asia/Calcutta, IST)

**Public health sources** (for M1 and the 1F check):
- NHS, Altitude sickness: https://www.nhs.uk/conditions/altitude-sickness/ (page states it was last reviewed 31 July 2023)
- CDC, Travel to High Altitudes: https://wwwnc.cdc.gov/travel/page/travel-to-high-altitudes
- CDC Yellow Book 2026, High-Altitude Travel and Altitude Illness (Hackett and Shlim): https://www.cdc.gov/yellow-book/hcp/environmental-hazards-risks/high-altitude-travel-and-altitude-illness.html. This chapter cites the Wilderness Medical Society 2024 guideline. I didn't open that guideline directly, and it isn't needed for the client-level wording above.

**RNLI** (for S4; opened 18 November 2026 for the multisport review):
- Cold water shock: https://rnli.org/safety/know-the-risks/cold-water-shock
- Float to Live, including rip currents and the "between the flags" advice: https://rnli.org/safety/float

**Google Books catalogue pages** (live, titles matched; catalogue text only, no interior pages read):
- Berger et al., *Mountain and High-Altitude Medicine* (2026): https://books.google.com/books?id=3GDsEQAAQBAJ
- Feehally, *Beastmaking*: https://books.google.com/books?id=111BEAAAQBAJ
- MacLeod, *Make Or Break*: https://books.google.com/books?id=VeLzrQEACAAJ
- The Mountaineers, *Freedom of the Hills* (10th ed.): https://books.google.com/books?id=IbsbEQAAQBAJ
- Hawkins et al., *Vertical Aid*: https://books.google.com/books?id=YrLUDAAAQBAJ
- Snyder, *Fit to Surf*: https://books.google.com/books?id=ly4IAAAACAAJ
- Snyder, *Fit to Paddle*: https://books.google.com/books?id=JQG5AAAACAAJ
- Hamlin and Hellemans, *The Ups and Downs of Altitude and Hypoxic Training*: https://books.google.com/books?id=YA7hEQAAQBAJ

**Not used:** UIAA Medical Commission pages. NHS and CDC covered every client-level point. A search-result fact sheet from a travel-medicine society was seen but not opened, so it isn't cited.

---

## Questions for Susmit

1. **Altitude warning signs.** Should 2D carry the short NHS and CDC warning-sign box (M1)? Or only the one-line "tell your guide, don't go higher, go down if worse" minimum? In both versions, no drug names, doses or ascent numbers appear.
2. **"Physio" in client-facing text.** Is there a qualified human physiotherapist behind the "Physio" name? If not, client-facing pages should say "a physiotherapist or doctor" and treat Physio as our internal teammate that adjusts the plan (M2).
3. **Clearance wording.** For clients who answer "yes" in 1F, do you want "wait for clinician clearance" (as section 7 says)? Or "you have spoken to your doctor and we follow their advice"? The second matches the approach in the Metabolic Health guide.
4. **Readiness.** Are you comfortable with an explicit "we don't certify or tell you you're ready for a trip" line (M3)? It may affect how retests and progress notes are worded to clients.
5. **Under-18s.** Should there be a standard line for school-trek or junior-camp enquiries, or should they always come to you first, as now?

*End of DRAFT review. Path: `/workspace/coaching-standards/review-outdoor-starter-pack-v1.md`*
`````

`````
# Coaching Standards review: Tactical Starter Pack v1 (safety-sensitive sections)

**DRAFT review for Susmit. Nothing has been sent to anyone, and the tactical file has not been edited.**

**Reviewer:** Coaching Standards (OnlineFitnessWorldwide)
**Date:** Friday 20 November 2026 (Asia/Calcutta, IST)
**File reviewed:** `/workspace/tactical-fitness-coach/tactical-starter-pack-v1.md`

**Sections reviewed**
- 1: intake, including 1A–1E (lines 42–100)
- 1F: clinician-first screening (lines 102–123)
- 1G: Physio screening and head-injury red flags (lines 125–138)
- 4A: load-carriage / ruck progression (lines 331–395)
- 4B: fire-and-rescue carry, drag and stair menu (lines 397–415)
- 5: RPE and autoregulation for shift weeks (lines 419–465)
- 6: "What we don't do" (lines 469–498)

**Also checked, only where they bear on the sections above:** 7 hand-off map (lines 502–522), 8 sources (lines 526–553). I also cross-checked the plan lines that schedule rucks (lines 207, 217, 235, 245, 253, 258, 291).

**Standard:** `/workspace/coaching-standards/coaching-standards-v1.md` (DRAFT v1): §2 scope map, §3 consent and confidentiality, §4 claims checklist, §5 safeguarding, §6 live-session safety and stop-and-seek-help signs.

**Context read:** `/workspace/tactical-fitness-coach/FIRST_JOB.md`, `READING_LIST_2026-11-20.md`, KB entry 16 (`/workspace/research/knowledge-base/2026-11-20.md`), persona `pending-bot-2026-11-20.md`.

> This is a standards check, not medical or legal advice. The suggested wording is for Tactical Fitness Coach to adapt. Public bodies (NHS, CDC, US DoD HPRC, JMVH) are cited as sources only. None of them endorses OnlineFitnessWorldwide, and citing a military health source does not imply any armed force's endorsement.

---

## Verdicts

| Section | Verdict | One-line reason |
|---|---|---|
| **Overall** | **Ready with fixes. Not for clients until the must-fix items are done.** | This is the strongest pack so far. Official-standard checks, no pass promises, no operational data collection, 18+, a hard boundary on fireground skills, and heat rules are all in place. Four gaps remain: no emergency tier for head injury; the Physio teammate is used as a clearance-giver; a trauma / sleep-disorder / disclosure boundary is missing; and section 6 lacks an emergency line, a fitness-for-duty line and an SCBA / live-fire line. |
| 1 Intake (1A–1E) | **Ready** (nice-to-have only) | Official-standard step (1A) is excellent. Lines 46 and 48 have a clear 18+ and no-operational-details rule. The emergency contact (1E) matches Coaching Standards §6. |
| 1F Clinician-first screening | Ready with small fixes (should-fix) | Conservative, with a clear "do not start" rule. The routing puts lung conditions under Metabolic Health, and there is no in-session emergency line. |
| 1G Physio / head injury | **Ready with fixes (2 must-fix)** | Has a "stop and see a clinician" rule but no emergency danger signs. Line 138 makes Physio a co-clearer. |
| 4A Load carriage / ruck | Ready with fixes (should-fix, plus the M2 wording) | Time-before-weight, gates, foot care, heat rules and a stress-fracture-aware stop-list are all good. The kg increments are unsourced, and "A common first load" reads as a fact. There is no cap on how often loaded sessions happen. |
| 4B Fire-and-rescue menu | Ready with small fixes (should-fix) | The hard boundary on casualty handling and fireground skills is good. Not stated: no SCBA or breathing-restriction masks, no live fire, smoke or heat gear, no ladders at height, and no dragging a person. |
| 5 RPE / shift weeks | Ready with fixes (1 must-fix) | Strong autoregulation and good "never" rules. It doesn't route sleep disorders, trauma, critical-incident stress, burnout or disclosures out to a qualified professional via Susmit. |
| 6 "What we don't do" | **Ready with fixes (1 must-fix)** | Covers weapons, combat, operational topics, no pass promises, no clearance after injury, no insignia and minors. Missing: fitness-for-duty / agency medical sign-off, SCBA and live fire, "not a substitute", an emergency line, and the mental-health boundary. |

---

## Must-fix (before any client use)

### M1. 1G (line 132): head injury has no emergency tier

> "Any knock to the head, or any concern about concussion, means we stop that work. The client sees a clinician. We do not assess, diagnose or manage concussion. Return to training only after medical clearance."

**Why.** Stop, see a clinician and return only after medical clearance is the right rule, and it matches KB entry 16's summary of Gagnon and Ptito. But it treats every head knock as "book a clinician." It never says when to **call emergency services now**. Coaching Standards §6 requires clear stop-and-seek-help signs. A live session with a pull-up bar, box or stairs can produce a fall.

**Public danger signs, checked 20 November 2026 (IST):**

- **CDC, Symptoms of Mild TBI and Concussion (adult danger signs).** Call emergency services or go to an emergency department right away for any of these:
  - headache that gets worse and does not go away
  - weakness, numbness, decreased coordination, convulsions or seizures
  - repeated vomiting
  - slurred speech or unusual behaviour
  - one pupil larger than the other
  - can't recognise people or places, confused, restless or agitated
  - loss of consciousness, very drowsy, or can't be woken
- **NHS, Head injury and concussion.** Call 999 for:
  - knocked out and not woken
  - can't stay awake
  - a seizure
  - vision or hearing problems
  - clear fluid from ears or nose
  - new numbness or weakness
  - problems walking, balancing, understanding or speaking
  - behaviour change

  Also: don't drive yourself to A&E after hitting your head, and see a GP if symptoms last more than 2 weeks.

**Suggested replacement for line 132** (awareness only, no treatment advice):

> **Head injury / concussion.** Any knock to the head, or any concern about concussion: **stop the session.** We do not assess, diagnose or manage concussion.
> - **Call your local emergency number now** if, after a knock to the head, the person: is knocked out or can't stay awake; has a seizure; has a headache that gets worse; is being sick repeatedly; has slurred speech, confusion or unusual behaviour; has new weakness, numbness or trouble with balance or walking; has vision problems or one pupil larger than the other; or has clear fluid from the nose or ears (CDC; NHS). Stay on the call if it is safe. Don't let them drive themselves.
> - **Otherwise, the client sees a doctor before training again.** Symptoms can appear hours or days later (CDC).
> - **Return to training only after their own clinician has cleared it.** Our team then rebuilds the plan inside that advice.

Add CDC and NHS to section 8 as "public safety sources (source only, no endorsement)." Do **not** copy NHS's ice-pack or painkiller lines (treatment).

### M2. 1G (line 138), section 7 (line 508), and lines 136, 387, 395, 415: the Physio teammate is given clearance and decision power (the same trap as the outdoor review)

> 138: "**Return-to-train after injury or illness** → wait for clinician **and Physio clearance**, then rebuild gradually."
> 508: "...keep only what **Physio agrees** is pain-free"
> 136: "→ **Physio**, and the client's own physiotherapist or doctor **as needed**."
> 415: "Pain in the grip, biceps, back, knees or Achilles → stop that pattern and **see Physio**."

**Why.** Physio is a teammate bot that drafts notes and safety reviews. It is not a clinician (Coaching Standards §2: clinical care stays with the client's physiotherapist or doctor).

- "Physio clearance" and "what Physio agrees is pain-free" give a bot a clinical decision.
- "Doctor as needed" makes the real clinician optional.
- "See Physio" in client-facing text reads as "see a physiotherapist."

Line 496 (section 6) already handles this correctly: "Physio... (through us)... still education and drafts, not clinical care." The rest of the pack should match it.

**Suggested replacements**
- **Line 138:** "Return to training after injury or illness → only after the client's own doctor or physiotherapist says it is OK. Our team (Physio teammate reviewing) then rebuilds the plan gradually inside that advice."
- **Line 136:** "Pain or injury history → the client's own physiotherapist or doctor first. Our Physio teammate then reviews the plan changes. We do not diagnose. We do not programme through pain."
- **Lines 387 and 395:** "Stop the loaded work. The client sees their own physiotherapist or doctor. Our Physio teammate reviews the plan. … Return to loaded work only after the clinician's advice."
- **Line 415:** "→ stop that pattern. If it persists or is sharp, see a physiotherapist or doctor; we adjust the plan to their advice."
- **Section 7, line 508:** "...Stop the aggravating work; keep only work that stays pain-free and within the clinician's advice. Physio reviews the plan change."
- Lines 163 and 370 (outside this review) use the same pattern. Apply the fix wherever the text can reach a client.

If a qualified human physiotherapist stands behind "Physio," say so explicitly instead (question 2).

### M3. Sections 5 and 7 (lines 421, 461–465, 512): no boundary for trauma, PTSD, critical incidents, sleep disorders or disclosures

**Why.** Section 5 targets serving shift workers: night shifts, overtime and short sleep. That group may raise critical-incident stress, trauma, persistent insomnia or excessive sleepiness, burnout, alcohol use, or thoughts of self-harm.

- Line 421 rightly says "not a medical sleep protocol," but nothing routes a sleep problem anywhere.
- Section 7 (line 512) gives Mindset "test anxiety, resilience, motivation" and says "if the topic looks like a mental-health concern, flag Susmit." That is good, but "resilience" for this group can drift into trauma work.
- There is no safeguarding line for disclosures of harm. Coaching Standards §5 makes these always flagged to Susmit and never handled by a bot alone.

**Suggested addition to section 5**, after "What we never do on tired weeks":

> **Sleep, stress and mental health: not ours to treat**
> - Ongoing trouble sleeping, falling asleep at the wheel or at work, or feeling exhausted most days even after days off → talk to your doctor or your service's occupational health team. We adjust training; we don't treat sleep problems.
> - Stress after a difficult incident, trauma, low mood, burnout, or alcohol or substance worries → a qualified professional, such as your doctor, your service's welfare or occupational health team, or a mental-health professional. Tell your coach if you'd like; we'll pause or lighten training and flag Susmit, who can help you find the right route.
> - If you are thinking about harming yourself or someone else, or you're in danger, **call your local emergency number** now.

**Coach-facing rule** (put in section 7 or the persona, not the client page): "Any disclosure of harm or risk → flag Susmit immediately (Coaching Standards §5). Don't probe, advise, or promise confidentiality we can't keep."

**Section 7, line 512:** change "Test anxiety, resilience, motivation" to "Test nerves, focus and motivation (performance psychology only)." Add a row: "Trauma, PTSD, critical-incident stress, sleep disorders, mental-health concerns, disclosures of harm → **Susmit** (then a qualified professional). Pause or lighten training; no advice from us."

### M4. Section 6 (lines 477–496): missing four lines

**Already covered:**
- weapons, combat, defensive tactics and operational topics (479)
- confidential details (480)
- recruitment, eligibility and medical-standard advice (481)
- no pass promise (482)
- swim tests (483)
- minors (484)
- no clearance after injury (485)
- no "make weight" (486)
- no insignia or endorsement (488)
- no sending or booking (489)

**Missing:**

1. **Fitness-for-duty / agency forms.** Line 485 covers clearance after injury, but nothing says we don't certify fitness for duty. Add:
   > - Sign off, certify or confirm that you meet a standard, or that you are fit for duty, deployment or selection. We don't complete agency medical or fitness forms, write letters of fitness, or tell your agency anything. Your agency's own testers and medical staff decide.
2. **SCBA, live fire, gear.** Add:
   > - Teach or simulate live-fire, smoke, breathing-apparatus (SCBA) or gas-mask work, heights or ladder work, water rescue, or casualty handling. Don't train in breathing-restriction masks, sauna suits or full protective gear on your own to "get used to it." Do that only in your service's supervised training.
3. **Not a substitute.** After "Learn those other things from," add:
   > **Our coaching is not a substitute for your agency's instructors, physical-training staff, occupational health or medical teams.** Standards, kit and rules differ by country, agency and year.
4. **Emergency, and the mental-health boundary.** Add:
   > **In an emergency,** call your local emergency number. Numbers differ by country, so know yours. We can't respond in real time outside a live call. On a live call, if you become unwell, we stop and stay with you while you or someone with you gets help.
   >
   > We also don't provide counselling, trauma or sleep-disorder care. See the sleep, stress and mental-health note in section 5.

**Also fix line 484.** It says "unless Susmit and Coaching Standards have explicitly agreed a path." Coaching Standards drafts and reviews; it doesn't approve offers. Change to "unless Susmit has explicitly agreed a path, after a safeguarding review (Coaching Standards flags)."

---

## Should-fix

**S1. 1F (line 120) and section 7 (line 509): lung conditions and "any medical condition" are routed to Metabolic Health.**
Metabolic Health covers diabetes, blood pressure and cardiometabolic health. Asthma or other lung conditions, epilepsy and other conditions fall outside that scope.

Replace line 120 with:
> "Medical condition or medication → the client's own doctor or care team first. Our team then routes: **Metabolic Health** for diabetes, blood pressure or heart-health; **Women's Health** for pregnancy and postpartum; **Masters** for roughly 40+; **Physio teammate** for injury; anything else to **Susmit**."

Mirror this in line 509.

**S2. 1F (line 119): add the in-session emergency path.**
The screening rule ("do not start; see your own doctor") is right. Add one line:
> "If chest pain, fainting, severe breathlessness or collapse happens **during** a session: stop, call the local emergency number (or have someone with them call), and stay on the call (Coaching Standards §6)."

**S3. 4A (lines 338–339, 355, 357): unsourced load numbers, and one phrased as fact.**
- "**A common** first load … is about 5–8 kg" reads as a claim about common practice. No source was opened for it.
  - Change to: "**Our starting suggestion**, once walking is comfortable: about 5–8 kg (11–18 lb), lighter for smaller or less-trained clients."
- "About 2–3 kg at a time" is a reasonable coaching step, but no public source supports a specific kilogram increment. The sources I opened describe the *principle* only: light at first, then progressing.
  - Keep the numbers labelled as coaching starting points, per lines 19 and 333.
  - Add "Physio teammate to review."
  - Don't present them as safe limits.
- Line 337 and stage 3 ("drop time slightly when you add load") match the JMVH review: increase duration or distance "not at the same time as" an increase in load. Cite it.

**S4. 4A: no cap on how often loaded sessions happen.**
The plans add a weekly "light ruck" (lines 217, 253, 258), and 4A sets no frequency.

- US DoD HPRC, citing the Army Public Health Center:
  - limit ruck marches to one every 10–14 days;
  - allow 2–3 days to recover;
  - you can't "cram" for a march.
- JMVH narrative review (Coyle, Pope and Orr):
  - two to four evenly spaced load-carriage sessions per month;
  - loads light at first and progressing;
  - duration and distance increased, but not at the same time as load;
  - planned recovery.

Suggested addition to 4A Principles:
> "Space the loaded sessions out. Longer or heavier rucks: about one every 10–14 days, with easier days after (HPRC; JMVH). Short, light pack walks can sit between them only if feet, shins and sleep are calm. Don't add extra rucks to 'catch up' before a test."

Phrase both as sources, not endorsements. Physio teammate to confirm this fits the 3A/3B templates.

**S5. 4A (line 360): packing cue. Attribute it, and note the terrain caveat.**
"Keep the load close to the back and fairly high" matches HPRC for flat, stable ground. HPRC says heavier items go lower on unstable or rough terrain. Add "(on flat ground; lower and close on rough ground — HPRC)." A hip-belt note is also in HPRC (it reduces shoulder pressure and arm numbness). That's optional, since it's general packing, not field craft.

**S6. 4B (lines 403–411): spell out the gear and environment limits** (mirror M4 point 2 inside the menu):
> "No breathing-restriction or gas masks, no sauna suits or full protective gear, no smoke or heat, no ladders or working at height, and never drag or carry a real person. Drags only on a clear, non-slip floor away from stairs and traffic. Improvised loads (bags, jugs) must have secure handles; if a handle could fail, use a sandbag or pack instead. Stairwells: use the handrail, keep the route clear, and descend unloaded or lighter at first."

**S7. Section 6, line 487: anti-doping goes to the right place.**
Nutrition can educate on supplements. Whether a product or medication is allowed is a question for the client's agency rules and the relevant anti-doping organisation, and for their doctor if it's a medication.

Change to:
> "...go to Nutrition (education only). Check any supplement or medicine against your agency's rules and the relevant anti-doping body, and ask your doctor about medicines."

**S8. Section 7: add an under-18 row.**
The section 7 map has no row for minors. Add: "Possible under-18 / cadet / junior applicant → **Susmit** (Coaching Standards flags); do not start a plan." Lines 46 and 484 cover this in the text already.

**S9. Section 8: add the public safety sources.**
If M1 and S4 are adopted, add CDC, NHS, HPRC and JMVH under "Public safety sources (source only; no endorsement by any health service, armed force or agency)."

---

## Nice-to-have

- **N1. 1E (line 98):** at the start of each live session, ask where the client is training today (indoor/outdoor, alone or with someone nearby) and whether they know the local emergency number. This mirrors the Coaching Standards §6 screening prompts. No addresses or location data needed.
- **N2. Section 5, line 453:** add "After a night shift, don't drive to a session if you're drowsy" as a coaching safety line. This is a general road-safety point; source it if kept, or label it as a house rule.
- **N3. Section 5, lines 429–435:** the RPE-to-reps-in-reserve mapping is consistent with the RIR-based RPE model the pack attributes to Helms et al. (catalogue summary only). Fine as is.
- **N4. 1C (line 76):** "No judgement. This is not a pass/fail story." Good tone. Keep it.
- **N5. 1B (line 68) and section 6 (line 483):** the swim-test route points to Triathlon Coach's water-safety rules. Those rules have open must-fix items from my 18 November review (`review-multisport-open-water-v1.md`). Make sure the fixed version is the one referenced.

---

## Checks that passed

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Brand | OnlineFitnessWorldwide / Online Fitness Worldwide only. The whole-file grep for the retired brand name found nothing. |
| Prices | None. No currency symbols. |
| Pass guarantees | None. Lines 8, 61, 482 and 325 are explicit that no pass or selection is promised. |
| Official standards | Never claimed as current. 1A makes confirming with the agency the first step (lines 13, 55–56, 61). Specific test thresholds don't appear in the reviewed sections. |
| Endorsement | Clear disclaimers (lines 16, 399, 488, section 8), including FDNY, armed forces, police and fire, NSCA, ACSM, ASCA and ACE. No insignia. |
| Invented stats, results or stories | None. Plan numbers are labelled as coaching starting points (lines 19, 333, 553). |
| Worldwide framing | Good. UK, US, Australia, Canada and Europe test *types*; time zones from ET to AEST; heat examples across regions. India is "one market, never the default" (line 12). |
| Operational security and privacy | Strong. Line 48 says don't ask for operational, unit or deployment details. Line 53 says no unit identifiers or badge numbers. Line 480 asks clients not to send them. Privacy & Data Care is flagged for scores, videos and wearables (lines 57, 100, 459, 520). |
| Weapons, combat, self-defence | Excluded (lines 8, 479). |
| Safeguarding | 18+ only. Under-18s and cadets stop and are flagged (lines 7, 46, 484). |
| Heat | 4A heat rules (lines 372–383) correctly adopt the Coaching Standards rule ("stop-and-seek-help, not managed remotely"). No fluid volumes, salt tablets or cooling protocols. |
| Shift-week autoregulation | Conservative. No max efforts after short nights, no fatigue as a toughness test, no cutting sleep or fluids (lines 447, 461–465). |
| Cited URLs | All 27 Google Books IDs in section 8 resolve, and the titles match (checked today). KB-entry-16 attributions for Gagnon and Ptito, Easter, Malley and Spierer, and Helms et al. match the KB takeaways. |

## Sources checked for this review (opened 20 November 2026, Asia/Calcutta, IST)

## Questions for Susmit

1. **Physio wording (same as the outdoor review).** Is there a qualified human physiotherapist behind "Physio"? If not, every client-facing line should say "your own physiotherapist or doctor," with Physio as the internal teammate that adjusts the plan (M2).
2. **Mental-health and safeguarding route.** For serving personnel who raise critical-incident stress, trauma, sleep problems or self-harm, do you want a standard "flag Susmit → their own doctor / service welfare or occupational health / mental-health professional" route? Do you want a holding message drafted for you to approve (M3)?
3. **Ruck frequency.** Should loaded sessions follow the "heavier rucks about every 10–14 days" spacing from HPRC and JMVH? That would change the weekly "light ruck" in 3A and 3B (S4).
4. **Fitness-for-duty line.** Are you comfortable with an explicit "we don't certify fitness for duty or complete agency forms" line (M4)? It may affect how progress notes and test-day checklists are worded.
5. **Under-18 path.** Line 484 suggests a future cadet or junior path "if Susmit and Coaching Standards agree." Do you want that door left open, or should it read "not offered" for now?
`````

`````
# Reading list — Community — 2026-11-20

From OnlineFitnessWorldwide Google Books research (KB entry 16: /workspace/research/knowledge-base/2026-11-20.md). Brand: Online Fitness Worldwide only. Worldwide audience; never India-first. No prices, no invented results, pass rates, reviews or testimonials, no implied ASCA/ACE/NSCA/armed forces/police/fire service/race organiser or other body's endorsement. Education only, not medical, dietetic, legal or recruitment advice.

- [The Live-Streaming Handbook: How to create live video for social media on your phone and desktop](https://books.google.com/books?id=ktZCDwAAQBAJ) — Peter Stewart  
  Why it matters: Live member Q&A and follow-along formats across time zones.
- [Smartphone Video Storytelling](https://books.google.com/books?id=cGNgDwAAQBAJ) — Robb Montgomery  
  Why it matters: Member story clips (consent first).
- [Spartan Fit! 30 Days. Transform Your Mind. Transform Your Body. Commit to Grit.](https://books.google.com/books?id=zt8MBgAAQBAJ) — Joe De Sena, John Durant  
  Why it matters: Virtual 30-day challenge ideas (no in-person events).
`````

`````
# Reading list — Hybrid Race Coach — 2026-11-20

From OnlineFitnessWorldwide Google Books research (KB entry 16: /workspace/research/knowledge-base/2026-11-20.md). Brand: Online Fitness Worldwide only. Worldwide audience; never India-first.   Why it matters: OCR grip and balance demands.

  Why it matters: OCR planning for busy amateur athletes.
`````
