`````
## 1. Tactical-goal intake checklist `[REVIEW: Coaching Standards]` `[REVIEW: Physio]`

Use on the consultation call or as a pre-call form. Short answers are fine.

Before any plan: the official-standard check (1A), clinician-first screens (1F) and Physio screens (1G) must be complete. We coach adults only. If the person may be under 18 (including cadets and under-18 applicants), stop and flag Susmit and Coaching Standards. Do not start a plan.

Ask only what we need to coach safely. Do **not** ask for operational, unit, deployment, or employer-confidential details.

### 1A. Confirm the current official standard (do this first)

- [ ] What is the **named test**, in the client's words? (The public test name is enough.)
- [ ] Which **country** and which **service or agency type** (for example: army applicant, police, fire and rescue, ambulance, coastguard, reservist, corrections)? We do not need unit identifiers, badge numbers, or anything confidential.
- [ ] **Test date**, or the window they are aiming for. How firm is it?
- [ ] Has the client **read the current official standard and protocol** from their own agency (events, order, distances, loads, rest rules, footwear and kit, scoring, retake rules)?
- [ ] If not: the first homework is to obtain that document from the agency and bring the event list back. We do not guess official standards from a book, a forum, or an older version of a test.
- [ ] Current scores, times or stage reached, **if known and if the client chooses to share them**. These stay inside the plan as coaching notes, not as a report or dashboard. `[REVIEW: Privacy & Data Care]`

State this clearly to the client:

> We will prepare you for the events in your current official standard. You confirm that standard with your agency. We do not promise a pass or a selection decision.

### 1B. Role and goal (physical work only)

- [ ] Applicant, serving member, reservist, or returning after time away (injury, illness or parental leave)?
- [ ] Which physical events matter most, from the official list (for example: shuttle run, timed run, pull-ups, push-ups, trunk event, drag, carry, stairs, ladder, loaded march, pool swim)?
- [ ] Any event the client is most unsure about?
- [ ] Swim event? If yes, pool-based education only, under Triathlon Coach's water-safety rules. We never supervise a swim test. (See section 6.)

### 1C. Training history

- [ ] What do you do now, and how often each week?
- [ ] Strength training: none / some / regular. Any current plan with **Strength Coach**?
- [ ] Running, shuttle, swimming, rucking or sport in the last 3 months? Longest recent session?
- [ ] Are you already working with Run Coach, Triathlon Coach, Hybrid Race Coach, Masters, Women's Health or Physio? (We fit around that plan.)
- [ ] Previous test attempts, if they wish to say: what felt hardest? No judgement. This is not a pass/fail story.

### 1D. Shift pattern, time and climate

- [ ] Your time zone. We book in your clock — for example ET, CET, GST, SGT, AEST, IST, or wherever you are.
- [ ] Shift pattern: days, nights, rotating roster, overtime, on-call, or regular days?
- [ ] When do you usually sleep after a night shift? When do you honestly have energy to train?
- [ ] Sessions per week you can really do. Length of each.
- [ ] Equipment: bodyweight only / home kit / gym access? Tick what you have:
  - [ ] Floor space and a mat
  - [ ] A sturdy pull-up bar, rings or a strong table for rows
  - [ ] Dumbbells, kettlebells, a barbell, or a sandbag
  - [ ] A backpack you can load
  - [ ] Stairs, a step 15–40 cm (6–16 in), a hill, or a treadmill
  - [ ] A sled, or a towel/blanket you can drag on a safe floor
  - [ ] A pool you can use with appropriate supervision (for swim-test education only)
- [ ] Climate where you train now (hot, humid, cold, wet, dry, altitude if relevant). Outdoor or indoor?

### 1E. Emergency contact (live-session safety)

Ask at onboarding, in line with Coaching Standards v1:

- [ ] Name, relationship and phone number of someone we may contact if you become unwell on a live call.
- [ ] Confirm you have that person's permission to share their details, and keep them up to date.
- Exact form fields and storage rules are for Susmit, Privacy & Data Care, and a qualified adviser. We only establish that the question is asked. `[REVIEW: Privacy & Data Care]`

### 1F. Clinician-first screening (prompts, not a clearance form) `[REVIEW: Coaching Standards]` `[REVIEW: Physio]`

These are plain-language prompts, not a diagnosis tool and not a formal medical-clearance form. The formal onboarding form remains an open question for Susmit (Coaching Standards v1).

Ask, and ask again when anything changes:

- How are you feeling compared with usual?
- Has a doctor ever told you to exercise only under medical supervision, or to avoid it?
- Do you get chest pain, pressure or discomfort — at rest or when active?
- Have you fainted, blacked out, or lost balance from dizziness recently?
- Do you get unusual shortness of breath for the effort?
- Do you have a heart, blood pressure, lung, metabolic or other medical condition, or take medication for one?
- Are you pregnant, or have you given birth in the last year? Any pelvic-health concerns?
- Is there anything else you think we should know before you train?

**What we do with the answers**

- Chest pain, fainting, dizziness, unusual breathlessness, or a known heart condition → do not start. The client sees their own doctor first. Flag Susmit.
- Medical condition or medication → **Metabolic Health**, and the client's own care team. Clinician first. We only adapt training inside that advice.
- Pregnancy or postpartum → **Women's Health**, and the client's midwife, doctor or pelvic-health physiotherapist. Clinician first. We do not start a test-prep plan without that clearance.
- Clients roughly 40+ → co-plan with **Masters**.
- If the client already has limits from their clinician, we work inside them. We never override them.

### 1G. Physio screening, including head-injury red flags `[REVIEW: Physio]` `[REVIEW: Coaching Standards]`

- Do you have pain, a recent injury, surgery, or a joint, back, shin or foot problem that limits movement?
- Any history of stress fracture, recurrent shin pain, or foot pain that worsens with impact or walking?
- Any recent knock to the head, suspected concussion, or lingering symptoms after a head injury?
- Are you returning to a test after injury, illness or a clinician-led lay-off?

**Head injury / concussion.** Any knock to the head, or any concern about concussion, means we stop that work. The client sees a clinician. We do not assess, diagnose or manage concussion. Return to training only after medical clearance.

This rule is informed by Gagnon and Ptito, *Sports Concussions: A Complete Guide to Recovery and Management* (KB entry 16 catalogue summary: sport-related concussion is a complex injury with very individual effects; the book is clinician-level background for Physio only). Citing it is a source citation. It is not a treatment protocol, and it does not mean any author or publisher endorses OnlineFitnessWorldwide.

**Pain or injury history** (including from previous rucking, running, or job-simulation practice) → **Physio**, and the client's own physiotherapist or doctor as needed. We do not diagnose. We do not programme through pain.

**Return-to-train after injury or illness** → wait for clinician and Physio clearance, then rebuild gradually.

---

## 2. Test-to-qualities map

A fitness test is a list of events. We train the qualities those events demand. We do not memorise an old scores table and hope it is still current.

The event-by-event idea follows Palin and Hartman, *Preparing for the Army Combat Fitness Test* (KB entry 16: a multi-event test maps onto trainable qualities; the book is a *model* for breaking any agency test into training blocks). Official, current events and scoring always come from the client's agency. Protocols change.

Needs analysis and programme design sit against NSCA (Alvar, Sell and Dawes), *NSCA's Essentials of Tactical Strength and Conditioning* (2nd ed.) (KB entry 16: movement patterns and programme design for occupational demands). Citing this textbook never implies NSCA endorsement of OnlineFitnessWorldwide.

For complete beginners, Murphy, *The Official British Army Fitness Guide* (KB entry 16) is our reminder to progress from the ground up, in a UK-inclusive, worldwide frame — not a US-only one. Citing it never implies endorsement by the British Army, the Army Physical Training Corps, or any other service.

Field-testing practice (how to test, then prescribe, then retest at sensible intervals) is informed by Heyward and Gibson, *Advanced Fitness Assessment and Exercise Prescription*; Hagerman, *Fitness Testing 101*; and Coulson and Archer, *Practical Fitness Testing* (KB entry 12, 2026-11-16). Tests in this pack are coaching tools, not lab diagnostics and not a dashboard. Citing NSCA's *Guide to Tests and Assessments* (Miller, ed.), if we use it for remote-safe test choice, never implies NSCA endorsement.

### How common test types map to qualities

Use the client's official event list. The rows below are *types* seen across several countries and continents, not a claim about any agency's current standard.

| Test type (examples of where the *type* appears) | What it tends to ask for | Qualities we train | Notes and hand-offs |
|---|---|---|---|
| Multi-stage shuttle run / "bleep" test (used in many UK, Australian, European and other police, fire and military settings; distance and timing vary) | Repeated 15–20 m (49–66 ft) shuttles at a rising pace, turns, and the courage to keep going | Aerobic base; change of direction; work capacity | Confirm shuttle length, timing and end rule with the agency. Road running beyond the test goes to **Run Coach**. |
| Combat / multi-event fitness tests (for example the US Army Combat Fitness Test *as a published event-by-event model*: strength lift, power throw, push-up variation, sprint-drag-carry, trunk event, run — Palin and Hartman; Papple Johnston, *ACFT Army Combat Fitness Test For Dummies*, for plain-language explainer style only) | Several qualities in one session, with limited rest | Strength first; trunk; work capacity; aerobic base; sometimes grip | Event lists and scoring change. The client brings the current official list. No US Army endorsement is implied. |
| Job-simulation drag / carry / stair / ladder tests (common in fire and rescue applicant tests in the US, Canada, the UK, Australia and elsewhere; some police job-simulation courses use drags, vaults and carries) | Moving a load, climbing, changing direction under fatigue | Strength first; grip and trunk; work capacity; load carriage; change of direction | We train the *physical qualities* with gym, home or bodyweight tools. We do not teach fireground, rescue or arrest techniques. See section 4 and section 6. |
| Loaded marches / pack walks (used in many military and some emergency-service standards worldwide) | Walking or marching under a pack for a set time or distance | Aerobic base; load carriage; trunk; foot and tissue tolerance | Start unloaded, build time before weight (section 4). Pain → stop and see **Physio**. |
| Pull-up, push-up and trunk-endurance events (very common across forces and services) | Strict reps, a set cadence, or a timed hold, to a written standard | Strength first; trunk; sometimes grip | Regressions first. Cadence and hand position come from the official protocol, not from us. Video review of standards is an open question (section 9). |
| Pool-based swim tests (some naval, coastguard, police and rescue services) | Swimming, treading or in-water tasks in a pool, to the agency's rule | Aerobic base and swim skill, when relevant | **Triathlon Coach** owns swim-technique education, under that teammate's water-safety rules. We never supervise a swim test. No open-water occupational practice in our plans. |

### The qualities, in the order we usually build them

1. **Aerobic base.** Easy, repeatable work you can speak through. This is the foundation for shuttles, runs, marches and long job-simulation courses. Informed by Maffetone, *The Endurance Handbook*, and Fitzgerald, *80/20 Endurance* (KB entry 11, 2026-11-15): most people spend too long at a "sort of hard" pace; easy days should stay easy. Kenney, Wilmore and Costill, *Physiology of Sport and Exercise*, is background for how the body adapts to different intensities (no ACSM endorsement implied by that textbook's guidelines).
2. **Strength first.** A squat or step pattern, a hinge, a push, a pull, and a carry. Strength Coach owns the main strength block. We add only what the test needs.
3. **Grip and trunk.** Often the limiter on carries, drags, hangs and longer courses. See Burr, *Get a Grip*, and Slater and Campo, *Strongman Training for Strength and Performance* (KB entry 10, 2026-11-14). Obstacle-grip overlap, if any, is coordinated with **Hybrid Race Coach** (Magida and Rodriguez, *The Essentials of Obstacle Race Training*, KB entry 16).
4. **Work capacity.** The ability to repeat quality efforts with limited rest — intervals, shuttle sets, and short job-simulation circuits. Built on the aerobic base, not instead of it.
5. **Change of direction.** Turns on a shuttle, changes of direction on a job course. Teach short, tidy turns on a non-slip surface. No contact, no takedown, no "opponent" drills.
6. **Load carriage.** Walking and, later, marching or climbing with a pack or a carry. Section 4.

We scale every quality to the person in front of us: women, older applicants (with Masters), first-time trainers, and people returning after time away are all welcome. No hazing, no "smoke session," no glorifying pain.

---

## 3. Plan templates with regressions and progressions

All three templates are drafts. They fit around **Strength Coach's** main strength block: two strength sessions remain the default, unless Strength Coach and the client's week say otherwise. We add test-specific work (push-up, pull-up, trunk, grip, shuttle, carries, stairs, rucking) rather than writing a second full strength plan.

Units are metric first, imperial in brackets. Times and RPE work in any country.

**Effort key (used in every plan):** RPE 1–10, as a coaching scale inside the plan (see section 5). Easy aerobic is RPE 3–5 (you can talk). Quality work is about RPE 6–8. Near-max efforts (RPE 9–10) are rare, never used after a short night, and never used to "test toughness."

**Equipment key:** **BW** = bodyweight; **Home** = bands, a backpack, a step, household carries; **Gym** = barbell, dumbbells, cable, sled, or a stair machine, if available. Choose the column that matches the client.

Periodisation ideas (a longer applicant block, then sharper test work) are informed by Smith, *Tactical Fitness* (KB entry 16: a year-scale plan for people preparing for police, fire and military roles, with scaled calisthenics and cardio; swim parts stay with Triathlon Coach; nothing promises a pass). Time-efficient weeks for shift workers are informed by Androulakis Korakakis, *Train Smarter, Not Longer: The Minimum Effective Dose for Muscular Development* (KB entry 16). Beginner-friendly progressions take their spirit from Murphy, *The Official British Army Fitness Guide*. Event-by-event sharpening takes its structure from Palin and Hartman. Plan sessions below are ours, not copies of any author's programme.

If the official test date is sooner than the block, we tell the client honestly that preparation time is short. We still do not promise a pass, and we do not advise them on whether to apply or defer — that is their decision with their agency.

### Shared regressions and progressions

Use these in every block. Move one step at a time. Pain → stop and see Physio.

| Pattern | Regression (BW / Home) | Standard | Progression (Home / Gym) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Push-up (match the official hand position and cadence when known) | Wall or high-incline push-up; then knee or low-incline | Full push-up, clean lockout | Feet-elevated, or a pause at the bottom, only if the official event needs it |
| Pull-up / hang | Dead hang or scapular hang; inverted row under a table; band-assisted row | Band-assisted pull-up or jumping pull-up with a slow lower | Strict pull-up; then official grip and any pause the protocol asks for |
| Squat / step | Sit-to-stand to a box or chair; assisted split squat | Bodyweight squat or reverse lunge; step-up 15–25 cm (6–10 in) | Goblet or backpack squat; higher step 30–40 cm (12–16 in); gym squat or trap-bar, per Strength Coach |
| Hinge | Hip hinge with a dowel or broom; short-range glute bridge | Romanian deadlift with light kettlebell, dumbbells or a backpack | Gym deadlift variation, per Strength Coach and the official lift if there is one |
| Trunk | Dead bug, heel tap, side-lying hold | Front or side plank, bird-dog, curl-up | Official trunk event practised to the written standard, not to failure every session |
| Carry | Farmer hold in place; suitcase carry with light bags or water jugs | Farmer carry 20–40 m (66–131 ft) | Heavier or longer carry; sandbag hug; sled push/drag (section 4) |
| Shuttle / change of direction | Walk-in shuttles; short 10 m (33 ft) easy turns | Easy 15–20 m (49–66 ft) shuttles at a conversational pace | Timed shuttle sets well below test intensity, then closer to the official protocol |
| Aerobic base | Brisk walk 20–30 min | Walk–jog or easy run / bike / row 25–40 min at RPE 3–5 | Longer easy session 40–50 min, or a light ruck once section 4 rules are met |

### 3A. 12-week applicant block (complete beginner)

**Who it is for.** An adult who is new, or returning after a long break, with a test roughly 12 weeks or more away, and a confirmed official event list.

**Week shape (about 3–4 sessions, fitted around Strength Coach).**

- **2 strength sessions** (Strength Coach's main block, 35–50 min). We slot test-specific push, pull and trunk work at the end, 8–12 min, rather than adding a third heavy lift day.
- **1 conditioning session** (shuttle technique, easy intervals, or a short carry/stair menu).
- **1 easy walk** (later, a light ruck if feet are happy). This can be on a non-coached day.
- At least one full rest day. After a night shift, drop to easy walking or rest (section 5).

**Weeks 1–4 — foundation**

- Strength: patterns in the table, light to moderate (about RPE 6–7, 3 or more reps in reserve). Two or three sets, not to failure.
- Aerobic: 20–30 min easy, building toward 35–40 min. Walking is enough. A jog is optional, not required.
- Shuttle: one short technique block a week — perhaps 6–8 easy shuttles of the official length (often 20 m / 66 ft, but confirm). Walk the turns if needed. No max-stage test.
- Trunk and push/pull: regressions every strength day.
- Walk: 20–30 min, mostly flat, **no pack** until walking is comfortable and the feet are calm (section 4).
- Skill: read the official protocol together. Practise only the movement standards, never operational tasks.

**Weeks 5–8 — build**

- Strength: add a little load or a slightly harder regression, still leaving reps in reserve.
- Aerobic: 30–45 min easy, most weeks. One slightly stronger finish is enough (for example 4–6 × 1 min a little quicker, with full easy recoveries).
- Shuttle or change-of-direction: short sets at a pace the client could repeat, not a full test.
- Carries and stairs: introduce the section 4 menu at light loads. Job-simulation *qualities* only.
- Ruck: only if section 4 gates are met — start light, build time before weight.
- One "looks a bit like the test" session in week 8, submaximal. Stop well short of a collapse. Note how it felt, not just the score.

**Weeks 9–12 — more specific, then ease**

- Keep strength. Do not chase new lifting personal bests at the expense of test events.
- One test-quality session a week: a subset of official events, in a similar order, at a hard-but-repeatable effort (about RPE 7–8).
- **Week 10 or 11:** a self-run mock test (see 3D). Then a slightly easier week before the real date if the test sits in week 12.
- If the test is later than week 12, repeat a build week rather than stacking extra hard days.

**Beginner volume cap (coaching starting point, not a law).** Do not jump weekly walking/running/rucking time by more than about 10 minutes, or a short extra interval set, in a single week. Feet, shins and sleep set the real cap.

### 3B. 8-week "sharpen for test day" block

**Who it is for.** An adult who already has a base (they can complete easy sessions and the basic strength patterns without pain), with a named test about 8 weeks away, and a current official protocol in hand.

This is not a crash course and not a toughness test. If the base is thin, say so calmly and use 12-week beginner ideas at a higher priority than "sharpening."

**Week shape.** Two strength sessions (maintain, Strength Coach leads), one test-specific quality session, one easy aerobic or light ruck day. Shift weeks: see 3C and section 5.

| Week | Aim | Strength | Test-quality work | Easy / recovery |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Confirm events; set a calm baseline | Maintain, RPE 6–7 | Submaximal run-through of each event, or a light mock | Easy 30–40 min |
| 2–3 | Build the weaker events | Maintain | Two focused quality sessions across the fortnight (shuttle *or* run; carries/stairs *or* strength-endurance events) | Easy 30–40 min, or light ruck |
| 4–5 | Put events together | Maintain; avoid heavy singles after poor sleep | One combined session in a test-like order, still with generous rest | Keep easy days easy |
| 6 | Sharpen | Slightly lower lifting volume | Second self-run mock, or the hardest quality week — not both | Extra sleep preferred to extra work |
| 7 | Reduce | Short strength sessions, quality intact | Short event practice only (for example a few shuttle runs, a set of push-ups to the official cadence) | Easy walking |
| 8 | Test week | Brief, familiar activation only, 24–48 hours before if it helps the client settle | No new events, no new loads, no max training | Sleep, food, kit. See 3D. |

If a mock in week 1 or 6 shows a large gap on one event, we spend more time there and less on events already comfortable. We still do not promise that the gap will close.

### 3C. Two-sessions-a-week maintenance for serving shift workers

**Who it is for.** Serving personnel or reservists who need to keep a test-ready base around nights, rotating rosters and overtime. Minimum effective dose, not a full applicant block.

Informed by Androulakis Korakakis, *Train Smarter, Not Longer* (time-efficient muscular work) and by section 5 for sleep and RPE.

**The rule.** Two honest sessions beat five exhausted ones. Book live sessions on the client's clock, preferably on a day off or before a day shift — not straight after a night shift.

**Session A — strength + trunk and grip (35–50 min), on the better-slept day**

- Warm-up: 5 min easy move, then the patterns you will load.
- 3 or 4 strength patterns (squat or step, hinge, push, pull), 2–3 sets, about 2–3 reps in reserve. BW, home or gym.
- 6–8 min trunk and grip (plank or curl-up variation, a carry or a hang).
- Done. No extra "finisher" for its own sake.

**Session B — the quality the test still needs (30–45 min)**

Pick one main quality each week, and rotate:

- Easy-to-moderate shuttle or run intervals, or
- Carry / stair / step menu, or
- A short test-event cluster (for example push-up cadence practice + a timed walk).

Stay around RPE 6–8. Leave the session able to work later that day, if duty requires it.

**Optional, only if feet and sleep allow:** one easy walk of 20–40 min on another day, unloaded or with a very light pack.

**After a night shift, a short night, or heavy overtime:** Session A or B becomes an easy walk, mobility, or rest. Do not "make up" missed hard work by stacking it on the next tired day. Slide the quality session to the next better-slept window.

### 3D. Test-day checklist and self-run mock test

**Self-run mock test (the client runs this on their own, or we watch live if they ask and the space is safe).**

- Use the current official events, order, distances, loads and rest rules — confirmed with the agency.
- Practise in a safe, non-slip space, with kit the protocol allows. No traffic, no live roads for shuttles, no unsupervised water.
- Warm up first (below). Start a little inside yourself. This is a rehearsal, not a punishment.
- Write down scores, times and RPE for coaching use only. They are not official results. `[REVIEW: Privacy & Data Care]`
- If anything is painful, stop that event. See Physio. A mock is cancelled more cheaply than an injury.
- Video is optional and only with consent (section 9).

**Test-day checklist (client-facing draft)**

The night before and the morning of:

- [ ] Re-read the current official joining instructions (time, place, kit, footwear, what you may carry, ID).
- [ ] Sleep as well as the roster allows. Do not add a hard session "to stay sharp."
- [ ] Eat and drink as you usually would before a hard session. Fueling and hydration questions go to **Nutrition**. Do not start a new food, supplement or "making weight" idea. Do not train thirsty, and do not invent large extra fluid volumes.
- [ ] Pack only what the agency allows, plus whatever you legally and practically need for the journey (water, a simple snack, a layer, any clinician-approved personal item).
- [ ] If you feel unwell, injured, dizzy, or not yourself after a knock to the head — do not test. Contact the agency by their process, and see a clinician.

Warm-up (about 8–12 min, adjusted to the official setting):

- Easy movement (walk or light jog).
- A few rehearsal reps of the first events (for example a short shuttle, a few push-ups, a light carry) at a comfortable effort.
- Then start the test at a pace you can sustain. On multi-event days, leave a little in reserve early.

After:

- Easy walking and a normal meal. No "punishment" session.
- Note what felt smooth and what did not, if you want to, for the next block. Mindset can help with nerves before and after. We still offer no pass or fail commentary that sounds like a guarantee.

---

## 4. Load-carriage / ruck progression and fire-and-rescue carry/drag/stair menu

### 4A. Load-carriage / ruck progression `[REVIEW: Coaching Standards]` `[REVIEW: Physio]`

Informed by Easter, *Walk with Weight: The Definitive Guide to Rucking* (KB entry 16 catalogue summary: choosing a load, packing it, warming up, and progressing in a way that aims to prevent injury). The steps and numbers below are our coaching starting points, not a copy of that book and not any agency's march standard.

**Principles**

- Start with unloaded walking. Build **time and distance before weight**.
- Start light. A common first load, once walking is comfortable, is about 5–8 kg (11–18 lb).
- Add weight only in small steps, about 2–3 kg (4–7 lb) at a time, and only after the current load feels settled in the feet, shins, hips and back.
- Do not jump to a test load "to get used to it." Confirm the official load with the agency when the tissues are ready, not before.
- Pain is a stop signal. It is not a badge.

**Gates before a pack goes on**

- [ ] You can walk 30–40 min on a mostly flat route, at an easy pace, with calm feet and no shin or joint pain during or after.
- [ ] Shoes or boots are already comfortable on unloaded walks (see footwear below).
- [ ] No current stress-fracture concern, and no "push through it" shin or foot pain. If in doubt, Physio first.
- [ ] Heat, illness and poor sleep are reasons to delay a loaded session, not to "tough it out."

**Progression (coaching starting points)**

| Stage | Load | Time or distance | Surface | Goal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0. Unloaded | None | 20–30 min, build to 40–45 min, or about 3–5 km (1.9–3.1 mi) | Flat, even | Easy, repeatable walks |
| 1. First pack | Empty pack, then 5–8 kg (11–18 lb) | The time you already walk comfortably, often 20–30 min | Flat | Pack feels quiet on the back; feet stay calm |
| 2. Time first | Same load | Build toward 40–60 min, or about 5–6 km (3.1–3.7 mi) | Flat, then gentle hills if available | Time and distance at the same weight |
| 3. A little more weight | Add about 2–3 kg (4–7 lb) | Drop time slightly when you add load, then build it back | Mixed, still controlled | Settled before the next jump |
| 4. Toward the official demand | Only if stages 0–3 are comfortable | Toward the official time or distance, confirmed with the agency | As the protocol requires, practised safely | Preparation, not a pass promise |

Packing cues (general coaching, not a field-craft lesson): keep the load close to the back and fairly high; tighten straps so the pack does not swing; no sharp objects against the spine; start on a short, easy route.

**Footwear and foot-care education**

- Train in the footwear you expect to use, once it is broken in. Do not wear a brand-new boot on a long loaded day or on test day.
- Build boot time gradually on easier walks first.
- Socks should stay smooth (no wrinkles). Many people do well with a clean, well-fitting pair they have already walked in.
- Check the feet after every loaded walk. A "hot spot" (a warm, rubbed patch) is the warning before a blister. Stop and fix the sock, lace or insole if you can.
- Keep nails trimmed so they do not jam the toebox on descents or in a stiff boot.
- Wet socks and wet feet rub more. Change into dry socks if you can; dry the feet when you finish.
- Anything beyond simple prevention — broken blisters, signs of infection, numbness, or pain in a bone or joint — goes to a clinician and to **Physio**. We do not treat feet from a video call.

**Heat and climate** `[REVIEW: Coaching Standards]` `[REVIEW: Physio]`

Loaded walking in heat is harder on the heart and on fluid balance. This is why the same session can feel much heavier in a hot, humid or high-sun climate (relevant in, for example, US summers, the Middle East, Australia, South and Southeast Asia, and southern European summers — not an exclusive list).

Background: Casa (ed.), *Sport and Physical Activity in the Heat*; Périard and Racinais (eds.), *Heat Stress in Sport and Exercise*; and Cheung and Ainslie, *Advanced Environmental Exercise Physiology* (KB entry 8, 2026-11-12). The team rule in Coaching Standards v1 still stands: **exertional heat illness is a stop-and-seek-help situation, not something a coach manages remotely.**

Practical rules for rucking and job-simulation work:

- Ask about the training environment (indoor or outdoor, heat, humidity, sun, ventilation) before a loaded or high-effort session.
- In the heat: shorten the session, lighten the pack, walk earlier or later in the day, take more rest, and keep easy days easy. Hydration education goes to **Nutrition**. We do not prescribe fluid volumes, salt tablets, or cooling protocols.
- If the client feels unwell in the heat — dizzy, sick, confused, unusually weak, or "not right" — **stop**. Seek local medical or emergency help when signs are serious. Stay on the call if it is safe. No medication advice. See Coaching Standards v1, section 6, for the live-session path and for public heat-illness awareness signs.
- Altitude or recent travel is a reason to ease, not to push. We do not prescribe altitude plans.

**Pain → stop and see Physio** `[REVIEW: Physio]` `[REVIEW: Coaching Standards]`

Stop the loaded work and refer to **Physio** (and the client's own clinician) if any of these show up. This is a coaching stop-list, not a diagnosis:

- Pain that sharpens with impact and eases only when you stop — especially in the shin, foot, heel or hip.
- Pinpoint bone pain, or pain that is getting worse week to week rather than settling.
- Pain at night or at rest after walking or rucking.
- Swelling, numbness, tingling, or a foot that no longer feels normal in the shoe.
- A sudden calf, Achilles, knee or back pain that changes your walk.

Do not "walk it off." Do not add pack weight to "stay on plan." Return to loaded work only after clinician and Physio guidance.

### 4B. Fire-and-rescue carry, drag and stair menu `[REVIEW: Coaching Standards]`

Informed by Malley and Spierer, *Get Firefighter Fit* (KB entry 16: a multi-phase conditioning approach for absolute strength, aerobic capacity, muscular endurance, agility and flexibility). Citing it never implies endorsement by the authors, by FDNY, or by any fire service.

Grip and odd-object ideas also draw on Slater and Campo, *Strongman Training for Strength and Performance*, and Burr, *Get a Grip* (KB entry 10, 2026-11-14). BenShea and Bryant, *Tactical Strongman*, is an implement-progression idea bank only (use the methods, not the hype; no operational content).

**Hard boundary.** We train physical qualities — carrying, dragging, stepping and climbing under fatigue — with gym, home or bodyweight tools. We do **not** teach casualty handling, a fireman's carry of a person, hose or ladder *technique*, forcible entry, search, or any rescue or fireground skill. Those belong to the client's own service instructors.

| Quality | Bodyweight / home | Home kit | Gym | Regression | Progression |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Carry (farmer or suitcase) | Heavy shopping bags or water jugs, 15–30 m (49–98 ft) | Dumbbell, kettlebell or backpack suitcase carry | Farmer handles, trap-bar carry, or heavy dumbbells | Hold in place first; shorter distance | Longer distance, slightly heavier load, or a turn at the end |
| Front / hug carry | Bear-hug a packed bag, laundry bag or cushioned sandbag | Sandbag or pack held at the chest | Sandbag or odd-object hug | Lighter bag, shorter walk | Low carry into a short stair climb, if the official test uses both |
| Drag | Towel, blanket or a loaded bag dragged on a safe floor, walking backwards or forwards as space allows | Same, with a little more weight in the bag | Sled drag (strap or rope), light to start | Short, slow, upright posture | Longer drag, or a drag into a short walk, if the official course links them |
| Stairs / steps | Stairwell, 4–8 flights broken into easy sets, or step-ups 15–25 cm (6–10 in) | Pack-loaded step-ups; a sturdy box 20–30 cm (8–12 in) | Stair machine or stadium steps, controlled | Fewer floors, slower, no pack | Light pack first, then slightly faster stepping *after* form is tidy |
| Ladder / high-step pattern (physical quality only) | High knee step onto a stable box; split-squat hold | Same with a light pack | Step-up to a higher box 30–40 cm (12–16 in), if the client can control the descent | Lower box, hand support | Only the physical pattern — no ladder-raise practice |

**Session sketch (15–25 min, after a warm-up, about RPE 6–7).** Pick 3 items. Example: farmer carry 3 × 20–30 m (66–98 ft); bag drag 3 short reps; step-ups 3 × 8–10 each leg. Full easy rest between sets. Quality of the walk and the brace matters more than speed, especially in early weeks.

Pain in the grip, biceps, back, knees or Achilles → stop that pattern and see Physio. Do not add a "harder tool" to compensate.

---

## 5. RPE and autoregulation one-pager for shift weeks `[REVIEW: Coaching Standards]`

A practical guide for night shifts, rotating rosters, poor sleep and overtime. Scores and RPE live inside the plan. This is not a readiness dashboard and not a medical sleep protocol.

**Sources (catalogue-level, KB entry 16).** Helms, Morgan and Valdez, *The Muscle and Strength Pyramid: Training* — a clear model for explaining RPE / reps-in-reserve and autoregulated progression. Haile, Gallagher Jr. and Robertson, *Perceived Exertion Laboratory Manual* — background on perceived-exertion scales and self-regulating intensity. Androulakis Korakakis, *Train Smarter, Not Longer* — minimum effective dose when time and energy are short. Roenneberg, *Internal Time* (KB entry 8, 2026-11-12) — useful context for body clocks and the "social jet lag" of living out of step with them, which night and rotating shifts often create. Optional bar-speed idea: Garcia Ramos, *Velocity-Based Training*, used only as a coaching cue with Strength Coach (below).

### The scale we use

One language for strength and conditioning. Teach it on easy days first, not on test day.

| RPE (1–10) | Rough meaning | Strength cue (reps in reserve) | Typical use |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1–3 | Very easy | — | Warm-up, easy walk after a night shift |
| 4–5 | Comfortable, conversational | — | Aerobic base, easy ruck, recovery |
| 6–7 | Getting serious, still controlled | About 3–4 reps left | Most shift-week strength work |
| 8 | Hard, quality, still tidy | About 2 reps left | Planned quality sets, when sleep has been reasonable |
| 9–10 | Near-max or max | 0–1 rep left | Rare. Not after a short night. Not as a "wake-up" session. |

If the official test uses its own perceived-exertion or cadence language, we still use this scale in training, and we follow the official language on test day.

### How to adjust a shift week

Ask, at the start of the session: *How did you sleep? Where are you on the roster? How do you feel compared with usual?*

| How the week actually is | What we do |
|---|---|
| Reasonable sleep, day shift or a day off | Planned session at the planned RPE |
| A bit tired, movement still looks tidy | Keep the session, but drop the load a little, or stop each set 1–2 reps sooner, or cut a set |
| Night shift just finished, a very short or broken night, heavy overtime, or you feel unwell | Easy walk, mobility, or rest. No mock tests. No heavy singles. No new personal bests. No "I'll catch up tomorrow" stack |
| Several short nights in a row | Two minimum-effective-dose sessions (section 3C), or one, is enough. Protect sleep on the first proper night off |

**Scheduling cues**

- Prefer training before a day shift, or on a day off, in the client's own time zone.
- After a night shift, sleep first when the client can. Training comes after, and only if they feel themselves.
- Rotating rosters: move the quality session with the better-slept window. The calendar can slide; the stop-rules do not.
- If a live session starts and the client is clearly exhausted, we change the plan in the moment. That is good coaching, not a missed week.

**Optional bar-speed cue (with Strength Coach only)**

If the client and Strength Coach already use lifting velocity, a clearly slower-than-usual warm-up rep can be a reason to reduce that day's load. This follows Garcia Ramos, *Velocity-Based Training*, as a readiness *cue*, not as analysis. No app charts, no reports, no extra devices required. If any app stores client data, Privacy & Data Care reviews consent and storage first. `[REVIEW: Privacy & Data Care]`

**What we never do on tired weeks**

- We never use fatigue as a toughness test.
- We never encourage training through pain, dizziness, chest symptoms, or heat illness.
- We never ask the client to cut sleep, skip fluids, or "make weight" to stay on plan.

---

## 6. "What we don't do" safety page `[REVIEW: Coaching Standards]`

> **Flagged for Coaching Standards review.** Draft client-facing page. Coaching Standards reviews every line before use. Not a final policy until Susmit has approved it.

**We help you prepare your body for a fitness test. We do not run the test, the service, or your career.**

We are coaches at OnlineFitnessWorldwide. We draft training and education for adults. We are not your agency, not a clinician, and not a recruiter.

**We don't:**

- Teach or advise on weapons, combat, defensive tactics, restraint, self-defence, survival, navigation, interrogation-resistance, or any operational matter.
- Discuss tactics, deployments, or employer-confidential work. Please do not send us those details.
- Give recruitment, eligibility, medical-standard, legal or career advice. We will not tell you whether you "should" apply, whether you "will get in," or how an agency will decide.
- Promise or imply that you will pass a test or be selected. Progress is individual. Official standards sit with your agency, and they change. You always confirm the current official standard and protocol with them.
- Supervise swim tests, or teach water safety. Pool-based swim-test *education* is only under **Triathlon Coach's** water-safety rules. We never watch or run a swim test for you. We do not programme open-water occupational swims.
- Programme for minors, including cadets and under-18 applicants, unless Susmit and Coaching Standards have explicitly agreed a path. That is not a current offer.
- Diagnose or treat injuries, concussion, or any medical condition. We do not clear you to test after injury or illness — your clinician does.
- Encourage you to train through pain, dizziness, or heat illness, to train dehydrated, or to "make weight."
- Prescribe medication, supplements, or a clinical diet. Fueling, hydration, body-composition and supplement questions (including anti-doping and agency rules) go to **Nutrition**, and to your clinician when they are medical.
- Use military, police or fire insignia, or imply that any armed force, police service, fire service, ASCA, ACE, ACSM, NSCA or other body endorses us.
- Send, post, book or sign you up for tests, courses or anything else. Susmit reviews and sends.

**Learn those other things from:**

- **Your agency's current official standard, joining instructions and instructors** — for the test itself, kit, and any operational training.
- **Your own doctor, physiotherapist, midwife or other clinician** — for clearance, injury care, concussion, pregnancy, medical conditions and return to training.
- **A qualified swim teacher and the pool's own safety rules**, with Triathlon Coach educating only on technique in a pool setting.
- **Physio, Metabolic Health, Women's Health, Nutrition and Mindset** (through us) for the questions that sit in their lanes — still education and drafts, not clinical care.

**Why this matters.** A job-related test sits inside a real workplace and a real medical and legal process. Our job is the physical preparation. Their job is the standard, the selection, and your care.

---

## 7. Hand-off map

Match the persona. Tactical Fitness Coach drafts the test-prep plan; these teammates take the topics that are not ours. Every teammate still only drafts. Susmit reviews.

| Situation | Hand to | What we do meanwhile |
|---|---|---|
| Any pain, injury history, stress-fracture or foot/shin concerns from rucking or shuttles, return-to-train clearance, head-injury or concussion red flags | **Physio** (clinician first for clearance and care) | Stop the aggravating work; keep only what Physio agrees is pain-free |
| Medical condition, medication, cardiometabolic questions | **Metabolic Health** (clinician first) | Wait for clinician advice; adapt only inside it |
| Pregnancy, postpartum, pelvic-health or cycle-related questions before a test block | **Women's Health** (clinician first) | Wait for clinician clearance |
| Fueling around shifts and test days, hydration, body-composition questions, any supplement question (anti-doping and agency rules apply) | **Nutrition** | Note the question; no dietetic advice from us; never "make weight" |
| Test anxiety, resilience, motivation | **Mindset** | Keep training calm and steady; if the topic looks like a mental-health concern, flag Susmit |
| Main strength programme, and VBT or RPE methods in lifting | **Strength Coach** | Add test-specific pieces; agree weekly load so two plans do not compete |
| Clients roughly 40+ | **Masters** | Joint plan, gentler progressions |
| Road running beyond the test itself | **Run Coach** | Shuttle and test-run work can stay here; longer road goals go over |
| Swim technique and pool-based swim-test education | **Triathlon Coach** (that teammate's water-safety rules) | We never supervise the swim test |
| HYROX, DEKA, ATHX, or obstacle-course racing | **Hybrid Race Coach** | Share grip and carry ideas if useful; they own the race plan |
| Treks, mountain sports, long pack walks that are adventure goals rather than a service test | **Outdoor Adventure Coach** | Service load-carriage for a named test stays here |
| Safety wording, scope-of-practice, claims; all `[REVIEW: Coaching Standards]` sections in this file, including this pack's safety page | **Coaching Standards** | Draft stays unpublished |
| Client videos (push-ups, pull-ups, shuttles, carries), test results, wearable data; redacting employer or agency details | **Privacy & Data Care** (consent first; never ask for operational or employer-confidential information) | Collect nothing extra until rules are set |
| Virtual group challenges (no organised in-person tactical events) | **Community** / **Retention** | No "selection" framing, no insignia, no implied agency event |
| Turning notes into content, pages or ad drafts | **Video** / **Social** / **onlinefitnessworldwide.com** / **Paid Media** | Only after Susmit and Coaching Standards approve. No military, police or fire insignia, and no implied agency endorsement |

Cheung, Philip Ainslie, [*Advanced Environmental Exercise Physiology* (2nd ed.)](https://books.google.com/books?id=1BAVEAAAQBAJ); Till Roenneberg, [*Internal Time: Chronotypes, Social Jet Lag, and Why You're So Tired*](https://books.google.com/books?id=A6bqlvASfPAC)

**House rules followed:** `/workspace/brand-studio/brand-guidelines-v1.md` (name, worldwide framing, short coach voice, 10-point checklist, including ACSM in the no-implied-endorsement rule) and `/workspace/coaching-standards/coaching-standards-v1.md` (clinician-first scope, claims, safeguarding, live-session and heat rules). Persona: `/workspace/research/knowledge-base/pending-bot-2026-11-20.md`.

All notes on books come from KB catalogue summaries. No interior pages were read. No page numbers, quotes or statistics are attributed to any book. Plan numbers are coaching starting points written for this draft. No book is their source, and they are not results or official standards.

---

## 9. Open questions for Susmit

1. **Which countries' tests should we prioritise first?** This draft stays at the level of test *types* (shuttle / bleep, multi-event combat fitness tests, job-simulation drag/carry/stair, loaded marches, calisthenics and trunk events, pool swims) with examples from the UK, US, Australia, Canada and Europe. Which countries or service types should the next version go deeper on — still always with the "confirm the current official standard with your agency" step, and still without treating our notes as the official protocol?
2. **Units.** This pack uses metric first with imperial in brackets throughout (kg/lb, km/mi, m/ft). Is that the house preference, or should we follow each client's own unit choice in client-facing plans?
3. **Video reviews of push-up and pull-up standards.** Should we offer remote video reviews of event standards (live on the call, recorded clips, or both)? If recorded, Privacy & Data Care should set consent, storage, deletion and who may see the clip before we offer it.
4. **Client test results and wearable data.** How should scores, times, shuttle stages, and any wearable data (heart rate, sleep, load) be handled under Privacy & Data Care? Proposed default for discussion: the client shares a short summary in their own words or a screenshot they choose; we store only what the plan needs, with written consent; we redact employer and agency identifiers; we never ask for operational or employer-confidential details; nothing is used in content or teaching examples without written consent and your approval. Please confirm or amend.
5. **Programme fit (optional).** Do tactical test-prep blocks sit inside the existing live-online offers, or as a named block later? No prices in this pack either way.
6. **Formal screening form (optional).** Coaching Standards v1 leaves the formal onboarding / medical-screening form open. Should this intake (section 1) become that form for tactical clients, or stay a call checklist until the wider form is decided?


`````
